You see what he says: ‘I note your instructions re sending Vanderkemp to Florence, but he had not yet returned here from London, where I presumed he was staying with your knowledge and by your orders. When he arrives I shall send him on at once.’ What do you make of that, Inspector?”

===Chinese===

「探长，这桩事我越琢磨越不痛快。」他劈头就说。「我千求万求，但愿别真出了岔子。我知道的都会一五一十告诉您，不过在给您看斯霍夫斯的信之前，我最好先把这封信是怎么写起来的交代明白。」

他征询似的抬眼望过来，见弗伦奇点头，便接着说道：

「我想我先前提起过，范德肯普是我的外勤。欧洲各国的展销和拍卖，他场场都要去赶。他替我做成过好几桩顶大的买卖，论生意眼、论操守，我对他都信得过。我还同您说过，那批失窃的宝石，大半就是他经手买下、亲自带到伦敦来的。」

「这事您同我说过，先生。」

「近些年，范德肯普不出门跑的时候，就在阿姆斯特丹分号办事。可怜的格辛遇害前三四天，他刚从德国南部跑完一趟回来——他在那边向几家革命以来家道中落的旧贵族收购珠宝。三天前，确切说是上星期一，我得知佛罗伦萨（Florence）不久将拍卖一套极有名的收藏，当晚便写信给斯霍夫斯，吩咐他派范德肯普去意大利验看估价那批宝石，好让我酌量买下几件。这就是斯霍夫斯的回信，今儿早上刚收到。您瞧他说的：『来函示知派范德肯普赴佛罗伦萨一节已悉，惟其至今尚未自伦敦返回此地——我原以为他留驻伦敦，系出于您的知悉与吩咐。俟其归来，我当即刻遣其上路。』探长，您看这是怎么回事？」

===Original===

“Vanderkemp did not come to London, then?”

“Not to my knowledge. He certainly did not come here.”

“I should like to know why Mr. Schoofs thought he had, and also the date he was supposed to start.”

“We can learn that by wiring to Schoofs.”

Inspector French remained silent for a few moments. It seemed to him now that he had neglected this Dutch office. It was at least another line of inquiry, and one which might easily bear fruitful results.

The staff there, Mr. Duke had stated, consisted of four persons, the manager, a typist, and an office boy. There was also at times this traveller, Vanderkemp, the same Vanderkemp who was uncle to Stanley Harrington. It was more than likely that these persons knew of the collection of diamonds. The manager would certainly be in Mr. Duke’s confidence on the matter. Vanderkemp had actually purchased and brought to London a large number of the stones, which he had seen put into the safe, though, of course, it did not follow that he knew that they had been retained there. Besides, in the same way as in the London office, leakage of the information to outside acquaintances might easily occur. Inquiries in Amsterdam seemed to French to be indicated.

===Chinese===

「这么说，范德肯普没有来过伦敦？」

「据我所知没有。他确实没到这儿来过。」

「我倒想知道，斯霍夫斯先生凭什么当他来了；还想知道，他原定哪天动身。」

「给斯霍夫斯打个电报，便都问清楚了。」

弗伦奇探长默然有顷。此刻他才觉出，自己把这家荷兰分号冷落了。这至少又是调查的一条路子，而且未必不会结出果子。

那边的人手，据杜克先生所言，共是四人：经理、一名打字员，和一个小听差。此外还有时不时在的这位外勤范德肯普——就是斯坦利·哈林顿（Stanley Harrington）的那位舅舅。这几个人晓得那批钻石收藏的可能极大。经理在这件事上，必定是杜克先生的心腹。范德肯普更是亲手买下大批钻石运抵伦敦、亲眼看着它们放进保险柜的——当然，这未必就等于他知道那些钻石一直存放在那里。再者，同伦敦那边一样，消息走漏给外面的熟人，也是极容易的事。在弗伦奇看来，去阿姆斯特丹查访一番，已是势在必行。

===Original===

“I think I shouldn’t wire,” he said at last. “There is no use in starting scares unless we’re sure something is wrong. Probably the thing is capable of the most ordinary explanation. But I’ll tell you what I’ll do. I’ll slip across to Amsterdam and make a few inquiries. If anything is wrong I’ll get to know.”

“Good. I’d be very pleased if you did that. I’ll write Schoofs and tell him to help you in every way that he can.”

French shook his head.

“I shouldn’t do that either, if you don’t mind,” he declared. “I’ll just go over and have a look round. There is no need to mention it to anyone.”

Mr. Duke demurred, pointing out that a note from him would enlist Mr. Schoofs’ help. But French maintained his ground, and the merchant agreed to carry out his wishes.

French crossed by the night service from Harwich, and at half-past eight o’clock next day emerged from the Central Station into the delightful, old world capital. Though bent on sordid enough business, he could not but feel the quaint charm of the city as he drove to the Bible Hotel in the Damrak, and again as, after breakfast, he sauntered out to reconnoitre.

===Chinese===

「我想，电报还是不打的好。」他末了说道。「还没弄准出了乱子，先自惊动一场，没有半点益处。这事儿说不定有个再寻常不过的解释。不过我可以这么办：我悄悄过海去阿姆斯特丹一趟，私下查问查问。真有不对，我必能探出端倪。」

「好。您肯走这一遭，我再乐意不过。我这就写信给斯霍夫斯，叫他竭尽全力帮您。」

弗伦奇摇了摇头。

「要是您不介意，这一层也免了吧。」他声言。「我只过去转一圈看看。这事对谁都不必提起。」

杜克先生面有难色，指出有他一封短简，斯霍夫斯先生必肯鼎力相助。但弗伦奇坚持不让，这位商人只得依他的意思办。

弗伦奇从哈里奇（Harwich）搭乘夜班航船渡海，次日早晨八点半，走出中央车站（Central Station），踏进这座讨人喜欢的、古色古香的旧世界都城。此行虽然办的是桩谈不上体面的差事，坐车前往达姆拉克（Damrak）大街圣经旅馆（Bible Hotel）的一路，以及早饭后踱出门去四下踏勘的当儿，他都禁不住感到这座古城有一种奇妙的风致。

===Original===

Messrs. Duke & Peabody’s office was close by in the Singelgracht, a semi-business street with a tree-lined canal down its centre, and crouching at one corner, a heavily-gabled church with a queer little wooden tower not unlike a monstrous candle extinguisher. French had opposed Mr. Duke’s offer to write to the manager introducing him, as he did not wish any of the Amsterdam staff to be aware beforehand of his visit. He had on many occasions obtained a vital hint from the start or sudden look of apprehension which an unexpected question had produced, and he was anxious not to neglect the possibility of a similar suggestion in this case. He therefore pushed open the swing door, and without giving a name, asked for the manager.

Mr. Schoofs was a dapper little man with a pompous manner and an evident sense of his own value. He spoke excellent English, and greeted his caller politely as he motioned him to a chair. French lost no time in coming to the point.

“I have called, sir,” he began in a harsh tone, not at all in accord with his usual “Soapy Joe” character, while he transfixed the other with a cold and inimical stare, “with reference to the murder of Mr. Gething. I am Inspector French of the Criminal Investigation Department of New Scotland Yard.”

===Chinese===

杜克与皮博迪公司的写字间就在左近的辛格尔运河（Singelgracht）街上。那是一条半商半住的长街，街心一道两岸栽树的运河，街角蹲着一座山墙巍峨的教堂，顶上一座古里古怪的小木塔，活脱脱一只放大了不知多少倍的烛罩。杜克先生本要写信给经理为弗伦奇引见，被弗伦奇拦下了——他不愿阿姆斯特丹分号的任何人事先得知这趟来访。他曾不止一次，从旁人一开口的神气、或一个冷不防的问题骤然逼出的惶遽脸色里，捉到过攸关全局的提示，这一回他也不想坐失类似的机会。于是他推开弹簧门，不通名，不道姓，径直求见经理。

斯霍夫斯先生是个穿戴齐整的小个子，派头倨傲，一望而知颇把自己当成个人物。他英语说得极好，一面客客气气招呼来客，一面示意请坐。弗伦奇也不绕弯子，直奔正题。

「先生，我是为格辛先生被害一案登门。」他开口的腔调疾言厉色，同他素日「肥皂乔」（Soapy Joe）的那副做派全不相合，两道目光又冷又带敌意，把对方牢牢钉在原地。「我是新苏格兰场刑事调查部的弗伦奇探长。」

===Original===

But his little plot did not come off. Mr. Schoofs merely raised his eyebrows, and with a slight shrug of his shoulders contrived to produce a subtle suggestion that he was surprised not with the matter, but with the manner, of his visitor’s announcement.

“Ah yes!” he murmured easily. “A sad business truly! And I understand there is no trace of the murderer and thief? It must be disquieting to Londoners to have deeds of violence committed with such impunity in their great city.”

French, realising that he had lost the first move, changed his tone.

“It is true, sir, that we have as yet made no arrest, but we are not without hope of doing so shortly. It was to gain some further information that I came over to see you.”

“I am quite at your disposal.”

===Chinese===

然而他这点小算盘没有打响。斯霍夫斯先生不过扬了扬眉毛，肩膀微微一耸，便不着痕迹地透出一层意思：让他诧异的不是来客所为何事，而是来客说话的这副腔调。

「啊，是啊！」他从容地低声道。「真是件惨事！我还听说，凶手和窃贼至今杳无踪迹？那么大的京城，暴行作下了竟逍遥法外，伦敦人心里想必很不安生吧。」

弗伦奇明白第一着已失了先手，便换了口气。

「确实，先生，我们至今尚未拿获一人，但不日之内便可见分晓，这一点我们并未绝望。我渡海来见您，为的正是再搜罗一些情况。」

「我悉听尊便。」

===Original===

“I needn’t ask you if you can give me any directly helpful news, because in that case you would have already volunteered it. But it may be that you can throw light upon some side issue, of which you may not have realised the importance.”

“Such as?”

“Such, for example, as the names of persons who were aware of the existence of the diamonds in Mr. Duke’s safe. That is one of many lines.”

“Yes? And others?”

“Suppose we take that one first. Can you, as a matter of fact, tell me if the matter was known of over here?”

“I knew of it, if that is what you mean,” Mr. Schoofs answered in a slightly dry tone. “Mr. Duke told me of his proposed deal, and asked me to look out for stones for him. Mr. Vanderkemp also knew of it, as he bought a lot of the stones and took them to London. But I do not think anyone else knew.”

===Chinese===

「您手里有没有直接帮得上忙的消息，我无须动问——当真有的话，您早主动相告了。不过，也许有些旁枝末节，您自己未必觉察得出它们的分量，却未必不能借您一言而豁然。」

「比如说？」

「譬如说，有哪些人晓得杜克先生保险柜里存着那批钻石。这不过是许多条门路里的一条。」

「哦？还有呢？」

「不如就先从这条说起。实打实地讲，您能不能告诉我，这件事在您这边可有人知晓？」

「您要问的是这个的话——我知道。」斯霍夫斯先生答道，口吻略带干涩。「杜克先生同我讲过他筹划的那笔买卖，还托我替他留意宝石。范德肯普先生也知道，那批宝石里有不少就是他买下带去伦敦的。但我想，再没有旁人知道。」

===Original===

“What about your clerk and office boy?”

Mr. Schoofs shook his head.

“It is impossible that either could have heard of it.”

French, though he had begun inauspiciously, continued the interrogation with his usual suavity. He asked several other questions, but without either learning anything of interest, or surprising Schoofs into showing embarrassment or suspicious symptoms. Then he turned to the real object of his visit.

“Now about your traveller, Mr. Schoofs. What kind of man is Mr. Vanderkemp?”

===Chinese===

「您那位职员和小听差呢？」

斯霍夫斯先生摇了摇头。

「他们俩都不可能听说。」

弗伦奇虽出师不利，接下来仍旧拿出惯常的温文，把盘问续了下去。他又提了几个别的问题，却既没探得什么值得留意的东西，也没能让斯霍夫斯露出半点窘迫或可疑的形迹。于是他转到了此行真正的来意上。

「现在谈谈您那位外勤吧，斯霍夫斯先生。范德肯普先生是个怎样的人？」

===Original===

Under the genial and deferent manner which French was now exhibiting, Schoofs had thawed, and he really seemed anxious to give all the help he could. Vanderkemp, it appeared, was a considerable asset to the firm, though owing to his age﻿—he was just over sixty﻿—he was not able to do so much as formerly. Personally he was not very attractive; he drank a little too much, he gambled, and there were discreditable though unsubstantiated tales of his private life. Moreover, he was of morose temper and somewhat short manners, except when actually negotiating a deal, when he could be suave and polished enough. But he had been known to perform kind actions, for instance, he had been exceedingly good to his nephew Harrington. Neither Schoofs nor anyone else in the concern particularly liked him, but he had one invaluable gift, a profound knowledge of precious stones and an accuracy in valuing them which was almost uncanny. He had done well for the firm, and Mr. Duke was glad to overlook his shortcomings in order to retain his services.

“I should like to have a chat with him. Is he in at present?”

“No, he went to London nearly a fortnight ago. He has not returned yet. But I’m expecting him every day, as I have instructions from Mr. Duke to send him to Florence.”

French looked interested.

===Chinese===

在弗伦奇此刻摆出的和气与恭敬之下，斯霍夫斯已然冰消雪融，倒真像一心要倾力相助。据他所谈，范德肯普是商行一员不可多得的大将，只是年纪不饶人——他刚过六十——不能再像从前那样大干了。论人品，此人并不怎么讨喜：酒喝得略多，好赌，私生活方面也有些虽无从坐实、却不甚光彩的流言。加以性情阴郁，待人简慢，唯独真正谈买卖的时候，又能圆滑周到得可以。不过他也行过善事，譬如对他外甥哈林顿，就一向极好。斯霍夫斯和行里旁的人，谁也不特别喜欢他，但他有一样无价的天赋：宝石学问精深无比，估价之准，近乎邪门。他为商行立过功劳，杜克先生为留住这员干将，对他的短处也就乐得睁一只眼闭一只眼。

「我倒想同他谈谈。他眼下在吗？」

「不在，他快两个星期前去伦敦了，至今未归。不过我天天都在盼他，因为杜克先生有指示，叫我派他去佛罗伦萨。」

弗伦奇露出了关切的神色。

===Original===

“He went to London?” he repeated. “But I can assure you he never arrived there, or at least never reached Mr. Duke’s office. I have asked Mr. Duke on several occasions about his staff, and he distinctly told me that he had not seen this Mr. Vanderkemp since two or three weeks before the murder.”

“But that’s most extraordinary,” Schoofs exclaimed. “He certainly left here to go to London on﻿—what day was it?﻿—it was the very day poor Gething was murdered. He left by the day service via Rotterdam and Queenborough. At least, he was to do so, for I only saw him on the previous evening.”

“Well, he never arrived. Was it on business he was going?”

“Yes, Mr. Duke wrote for him.”

“Mr. Duke wrote for him?” French echoed, at last genuinely surprised. “What? To cross that day?”

===Chinese===

「他去伦敦了？」他重说了一遍。「可我敢向您担保，他压根儿没到过伦敦，至少没进过杜克先生的公司。我不止一回向杜克先生问起他的手下，他明明白白告诉我，案发前两三个星期起，就没再见过这位范德肯普先生。」

「那就太出奇了。」斯霍夫斯失声道。「他确确实实是从这里动身去伦敦的，日子是——那天是星期几来着？——正是可怜的格辛遇害的那一天。他走的是白天班船，取道鹿特丹（Rotterdam）和昆伯勒（Queenborough）。至少，他原是这么打算的，因为我只在头天晚上见过他。」

「可他始终没有到。他此行是为着公事吗？」

「是的，杜克先生写信唤他来的。」

「杜克先生写信唤他来的？」弗伦奇跟着重复，这一回是当真吃了一惊。「什么？要他当天过海？」

===Original===

“To see him in the office on the following morning. I can show you the letter.” He touched a bell and gave the necessary instructions. “There it is,” he continued, handing over the paper which the clerk brought in.

It was an octavo sheet of memorandum paper with the firm’s name printed on the top, and bore the following typewritten letter:

> “I should be obliged if you would please ask Mr. Vanderkemp to come over and see me here at 10:00 a.m. on Wednesday, 26th inst., as I wish him to undertake negotiations for a fresh purchase. He may have to go to Stockholm at short notice.”

The note was signed “R. A. Duke,” with the attendant flourish with which French had grown familiar.

===Chinese===

「是要他次日一早到公司相见。信可以拿给您看。」他按了按铃，吩咐了几句。「喏，就是这封。」他接着说，把职员送进来的那张纸递了过去。

那是一张八开的便笺纸，笺头印着商行的名号，上面打着一封如下的信：

> 「兹请转告范德肯普先生，务于本月26日（星期三）上午 10:00 过海来此一见。现有一桩新的收购，须请其出面洽谈；届时或须于短期间内动身前往斯德哥尔摩（Stockholm），特此先为知照。」

信末署名「R. A. Duke」，连着那笔弗伦奇早已看熟了的花体收势。

===Original===

He sat staring at the sheet of paper, trying to fit this new discovery into the scheme of things. But it seemed to him an insoluble puzzle. Was Mr. Duke not really the innocent, kindly old gentleman he had fancied, but rather a member, if not the author, of some deep-seated conspiracy? If he had written this note, why had he not mentioned the fact when Vanderkemp was being discussed? Why had he shown surprise when he received Schoofs’ letter saying that the traveller had crossed to London? What was at the bottom of the whole affair?

An idea struck him, and he examined the letter more closely.

“Are you sure this is really Mr. Duke’s signature?” he asked slowly.

Mr. Schoofs looked at him curiously.

“Why, yes,” he answered. “At least, it never occurred to me to doubt it.”

===Chinese===

他坐在那里盯着那张纸，想把这桩新发现安进整个事理的框架里去。可在他眼中，这成了一团解不开的乱麻。难道杜克先生并非他想的那样，是个清白和善的老绅士，而是一场深伏的阴谋里的人——若非主谋的话？倘若这张便条真是他写的，先前谈论范德肯普的时候，他为何只字不提？收到斯霍夫斯说这位外勤已经渡海去伦敦的信时，他又为何面露惊讶？这整桩事情，底下到底埋着什么？

他灵机一动，把信凑近细看。

「您能拿得稳，这真是杜克先生的亲笔签名？」他缓缓问道。

斯霍夫斯先生诧异地望着他。

「咦，是啊。」他答道。「至少，我从来没动过怀疑的念头。」

===Original===

“You might let me see some of his other letters.”

In a few seconds half a dozen were produced, and French began whistling below his breath as he sat comparing the signatures, using a lens which he took from his pocket. After he had examined each systematically, he laid them down on the table and sat back in his chair.

“That was stupid of me,” he announced. “I should have learnt all I wanted without asking for these other letters. That signature is forged. See here, look at it for yourself.”

He passed the lens to Schoofs, who in his turn examined the name.

“You see, the lines of that writing are not smooth; they are a mass of tiny shakes and quivers. That means that they have not been written quickly and boldly; they have been slowly drawn or traced over pencil. Compare one of these other notes and you will see that while at a distance the signatures look identical, in reality they are quite different. No, Mr. Duke never wrote that. I am afraid Mr. Vanderkemp has been the victim of some trick.”

===Chinese===

「那么，可否再让我看看他旁的信。」

不消几秒，六封信便摆了上来。弗伦奇从口袋里摸出一只放大镜，坐着逐一比对签名，齿缝间不由得低低吹起口哨。每一封都按部就班查验过后，他把信摔回桌上，身子往椅背上一靠。

「我真蠢。」他说。「原不必讨这些信来看，我要知道的便都能知道。那个签名是伪造的。来，您自己瞧。」

他把放大镜递给斯霍夫斯，斯霍夫斯接过去端详那个名字。

「您看，这字迹的笔画不圆滑，满纸都是细碎的抖动与颤笔。这就是说，它不是笔力酣畅、一挥而就写下的，而是照着铅笔底子一笔一笔慢慢描出来的。再拿这几封旁的信一比，您就会看出：远远望去，两个签名一模一样，实际上全然是两路。不，杜克先生从没写过这个。恐怕范德肯普先生是着了什么人的道儿。」

===Original===

Schoofs was visibly excited. He hung on the other’s words and nodded emphatically at his conclusions. Then he swore comprehensively in Dutch. “Good heavens, Inspector!” he cried. “You see the significance of all that?”

French glanced at him keenly.

“In what way?” he demanded.

“Why, here we have a murder and a robbery, and then we have this, occurring at the very same time.﻿ ﻿… Well, does it not look suggestive?”

“You mean the two things are connected?”

===Chinese===

斯霍夫斯显然激动了。他一字不漏地听着，到结论处便重重点头，接着用荷兰话结结实实咒骂了一通。「天哪，探长！」他叫道。「您看出这一切的干系了吗？」

弗伦奇锐利地瞥了他一眼。

「怎么个干系法？」他追问道。

「哎呀，这边是一桩凶案带一桩劫案，偏又添上这么一件事，前后正落在同一时刻上。……您说，这瞧着不是大有文章吗？」

「您是说，这两件事互有关联？」

===Original===

“Well, what do you think?” Mr. Schoofs replied with some impatience.

“It certainly does look like it,” French admitted slowly. Already his active brain was building up a theory, but he wanted to get the other’s views. “You are suggesting, I take it, that Vanderkemp may have been concerned in the crime?”

Schoofs shook his head decidedly.

“I am suggesting nothing of the kind,” he retorted. “That’s not my job. The thing merely struck me as peculiar.”

“No, no,” French answered smoothly, “I have not expressed myself clearly. Neither of us is making any accusation. We are simply consulting together in a private, and, I hope, a friendly way, each anxious only to find out the truth. Any suggestion may be helpful. If I make the suggestion that Mr. Vanderkemp is the guilty man in order to enable us to discuss the possibility, it does not follow that either of us believes it to be true, still less that I should act on it.”

“I am aware of that, but I don’t make any such suggestion.”

===Chinese===

「您说呢？」斯霍夫斯先生回道，透着几分不耐。

「瞧着确实像。」弗伦奇缓缓承认。他那颗活络的脑子其实已经在搭建一套设想，只是他还想先听听对方的见解。「照我理解，您的意思是：范德肯普说不定与这桩罪案有干连？」

斯霍夫斯断然摇头。

「我绝无此意。」他抢白道。「那不干我的事。这件事只是让我觉得蹊跷罢了。」

「不不，」弗伦奇婉转地答道，「是我话没有说清。我们谁也没有指控谁。我们不过是私下里——还盼是朋友似的——一起参详参详，彼此都只求查明真相。什么样的设想都可能有用。倘若由我来提出『范德肯普先生即是罪人』，好教我们便于讨论这种可能，那并不等于你我两人哪一个信以为真，更不等于我会照此行事。」

「这我明白，可我并没有那样的设想。」

===Original===

“Then I do,” French declared, “simply as a basis for discussion. Let us suppose then, purely for argument’s sake, that Mr. Vanderkemp decides to make some of the firm’s wealth his own. He is present when the stones are being put into the safe, and in some way when Mr. Duke’s back is turned, he takes an impression of the key. He crosses to London, either finds Gething in the office or is interrupted by him, murders the old man, takes the diamonds, and clears out. What do you think of that?”

“What about the letter?”

“Well, that surely fits in? Mr. Vanderkemp must leave this office in some way which won’t arouse your suspicion or cause you to ask questions of the London office. What better way than by forging the letter?”

Mr. Schoofs swore for the second time. “If he has done that,” he cried hotly, “let him hang! I’ll do everything I can, Inspector, to help you to find out, and that not only on general grounds, but for old Gething’s sake, for whom I had a sincere regard.”

“I thought you would feel that way, sir. Now to return to details. I suppose you haven’t the envelope that letter came in?”

===Chinese===

「那么由我来提，」弗伦奇声言，「纯粹是作个谈论的底子。我们不妨这样假设——单为立论起见——范德肯普先生决意把商行的一部分财物据为己有。宝石放进保险柜时他在场，趁杜克先生转背的当口，他设法拓下了钥匙的模子。他渡海到伦敦，或是在公司里撞见格辛，或是被格辛撞破，便杀了那老人，卷走钻石，一走了之。这个推想，您看如何？」

「那封信怎么说？」

「喏，那不是正好对上榫吗？范德肯普先生总得有个法子离开此地，既不惹您生疑，又不致教您去函伦敦公司查问。还有比伪造那封信更好的法子吗？」

斯霍夫斯第二次骂了起来。「他要真干下了这种事，」他气冲冲地嚷道，「就叫他上绞架！探长，凡是我帮得上的，我无不尽力，帮您把它查个水落石出——这不单是为着大道理，也是看在老格辛的分上，我对他是实心敬重的。」

「我就料到您是这个心意，先生。现在回到细节上来。装这封信的信封，想必是没有留下？」

===Original===

“Never saw it,” Mr. Schoofs replied. “The clerk who opened it would destroy it.”

“Better have the clerk in, and we’ll ask the question.”

Mr. Schoofs made a sudden gesture.

“By Jove!” he cried. “It was Vanderkemp himself. He acts as head clerk when he is here.”

“Then we don’t get any evidence there. Either the letter came through the post, in which case he destroyed the envelope in the usual way, or else he brought the letter to the office and slipped it in among the others.”

French picked up the letter again. Experience had taught him that typescript could be extremely characteristic, and he wondered if this in question could be made to yield up any of its secrets.

===Chinese===

「从没见过。」斯霍夫斯先生答道。「拆信的职员照例随手就毁了。」

「最好还是把那位职员请进来，我们当面问一问。」

斯霍夫斯先生猛然做了个手势。

「哎呀！」他叫道。「拆信的正是范德肯普自己。他在这儿的时候，就由他充任首席职员。」

「那么这一头是问不出凭据来了。那封信要么是邮寄来的——那样他照例把信封毁掉；要么他干脆把信随身带到分号，混进旁的信件里。」

弗伦奇重新拿起那封信。经验告诉他，打字的文卷往往极见个性，他寻思着，这一份能不能逼它吐出几分秘密。

===Original===

It certainly had peculiarities. The lens revealed a dent in the curve of the *n*, where the type had evidently struck something hard, and the tail of the *g* was slightly defective.

French next examined the genuine letters, and was interested to find their type showed the same irregularities. It was therefore certain that the forged letter had been typed in the London office.

He sat thinking deeply, unconsciously whistling his little tune through his closed teeth. There was another peculiarity about the forged note. The letters were a trifle indented, showing that the typewriter keys had been struck with rather more than the usual force. He turned the sheet over, and he saw that so much was this the case that the stops were punched almost through. Picking up the genuine letters, he looked for the same peculiarity, but the touch in these cases was much lighter and even the full stop barely showed through. This seemed to justify a further deduction﻿—that the writer of the forged note was unskilled, probably an amateur, while that of the others was an expert. French felt he could safely assume that the forged note had been typed by some unauthorised person, using the machine in the London office.

===Chinese===

它确有特异之处。放大镜下，字母 *n* 的弧弯上有一处凹痕，显见得那枚铅字磕过什么硬物；字母 *g* 的小尾巴也略有残缺。

弗伦奇接着查验那几封真信，饶有兴味地发现，它们打出来的字也带着同样的毛病。由此可见，那封伪造的信，必定是在伦敦的公司里打出来的。

他坐着深深思索，牙关紧闭，不知不觉又从齿缝里低低吹起他那支小调。那封伪信还有一处特异。字迹略微凹进纸面，可见打字机的键落下去时，手劲比寻常重了几分。他把纸翻过背面，只见情形竟重到这般地步：标点几乎要洞穿纸背。再拿起那几封真信检看同样的痕迹，它们落键却轻得多，连句号都几乎透不过纸背。这似乎又坐实了一层推论——写伪信的人是个生手，多半是外行，而旁的信出自熟手。弗伦奇觉得大可断言：那封伪信，是某个未获许可的人，用伦敦公司里那台打字机打出来的。

===Original===

But, so far as he could see, these deductions threw no light on the guilt or innocence of Vanderkemp. The letter might have come from some other person in London, or Vanderkemp might have typed it himself during one of his visits to the metropolis. More data was wanted before a conclusion could be reached.

Though from what he had seen of Schoofs, the Inspector thought it unlikely that he was mixed up in what he was beginning to believe was a far-reaching conspiracy, he did not mention his discoveries to him, but continued trying to pump him for further information about the missing traveller. Vanderkemp, it seemed, was a tall man, or would have been if he held himself erect, but he had stooped shoulders and a slouching way of walking which detracted from his height. He was inclining to stoutness, and had dark hair and a sallow complexion. His chin was clean-shaven, but he wore a heavy dark moustache. Glasses covered his shortsighted eyes.

French obtained some samples of his handwriting, but no photograph of him was available. In fact, Mr. Schoofs did not seem able to supply any further information, nor did an interrogation of the typist and office boy, both of whom spoke a little English, produce any better results.

===Chinese===

然而，就他所见，这些推论对范德肯普究竟有罪无罪，照不亮分毫。那封信也许出自伦敦的另一个人之手，也可能是范德肯普本人前几回到这座大都城时，亲手打的。没有更多的材料，结论便无从谈起。

探长虽就他对斯霍夫斯的观察而论，觉得此人卷进这场——他渐渐相信是——盘根错节的阴谋的可能不大，却没有把自己的发现对他明言，只管继续设法套问这位失踪外勤的情形。据称范德肯普个子颇高——倘若把腰板挺直了本可算高——只是双肩佝偻，走路拖沓，身量便打了折扣。他有发福的趋势，头发乌黑，面色灰黄。下巴剃得干干净净，唇上却蓄着一副浓黑的髭须。一双近视眼，架着眼镜。

弗伦奇弄到了几份他的笔迹样本，他的照片却一张也寻不出。说实在的，斯霍夫斯先生像是再提供不出什么了；那名打字员和小听差也都懂几句英语，可盘问下来，结果同样不见起色。

===Original===

“Where did Mr. Vanderkemp live?” French asked, when he thought he had exhausted the resources of the office.

It appeared that the traveller was unmarried, and Mr. Schoofs did not know if he had any living relatives other than Harrington. He boarded with Mevrouw Bondix, in the Kinkerstraat, and thither the two men betook themselves, French begging the other’s company in case he should be needed as interpreter. Mevrouw Bondix was a garrulous little old lady who had but little English, and upon whom Schoofs’ questions acted as a push button does on an electric bell. She overwhelmed them with a flood of conversation of which French could understand not one word, and from which even the manager was hard put to it to extract the meaning. But the gist of the matter was that Vanderkemp had left her house at half-past eight on the night before the murder, with the expressed intention of taking the 9:00 train for London. Since then she had neither seen him nor heard from him.

“But,” French exclaimed, “I thought you told me he had crossed by the daylight service on the day of the murder?”

“He said he would,” Schoofs answered with a somewhat puzzled air. “He said so most distinctly. I remember it particularly because he pointed out that Mr. Duke would probably ask him, after the interview, to start by the afternoon Continental train on his new journey, and he preferred to travel during the previous day so as to insure a good night’s sleep in London. He said that in answer to a suggestion of mine that he would be in time enough if he went over on the night before his interview.”

“What time do these trains get in to London?”

===Chinese===

「范德肯普先生住在哪里？」弗伦奇问。这时他自觉公司这一头，已再无可榨的了。

原来这位外勤没有娶亲，除哈林顿之外还有没有别的亲人在世，斯霍夫斯先生也无从知晓。他在金克街（Kinkerstraat）邦迪克斯夫人（Mevrouw Bondix）家里搭伙寄宿，两人便一道前往，弗伦奇还恳请对方作陪，以备需要他充当通译。邦迪克斯夫人是位饶舌的小个子老太太，英语只会一星半点，斯霍夫斯的问题对她，一如揿钮之于电铃，一按便响个不住。滔滔话语劈头盖脸涌来，弗伦奇一个字也听不懂，连经理本人都费尽气力才从中理出些头绪。不过事情的要旨是：案发前一夜八点半，范德肯普辞出她的家门，明言要赶 9:00 开往伦敦的火车。从此她既没再见过他，也没得过他只字音讯。

「可是，」弗伦奇失声道，「我记得您说过，他是案发当天搭白天班船过海的？」

「他是这么说的。」斯霍夫斯答道，神色有几分迷惑。「说得千真万确。我记得格外清楚，因为他特意点明：面谈过后，杜克先生多半会要他搭午后的欧陆快车踏上新的行程，他宁可头一天白天先过去，好在伦敦睡个囫囵觉。他这话，是回我的一个提议——我说他尽可以等到面谈前夜再动身，时间也尽够。」

「这几班火车，几时进伦敦？」

===Original===

“I don’t know, but we can find out at the office.”

“I’d like to go to the Central Station next, if you don’t mind coming along,” French declared, “so we could look them up there. But before I go I want you to tell me if Mr. Vanderkemp figures in any of these?” He pointed to a number of photographic groups which adorned the chimneypiece and walls.

It happened that the missing traveller appeared in one of the groups, and both Mr. Schoofs and Mevrouw Bondix bore testimony to the excellence of the portrait.

“Then I’ll take it,” French announced, as he slipped the card into his pocket.

The two men next went to the Central Station and looked up the trains. They found that the day service did not reach Victoria until 10:05 p.m. The significance of this was not lost upon French. Orchard stated he had reached the office in Hatton Garden at 10:15, and that it could not have been later was established by the evidence of Constable Alcorn. The body at that time was cold, so that the crime must have taken place some considerable time earlier. A man, therefore, who had crossed by the daylight service from Amsterdam could not possibly have had time to commit the murder. Had Vanderkemp lied deliberately to Schoofs when he told him he was using that daylight service? If so, was it in order to establish an alibi? Had he a secret appointment with Gething for an earlier hour on the fatal evening, and had he crossed the night before with the object of keeping it? French felt these were questions which required satisfactory answers, and he made a mental note not to rest until he had found them.

===Chinese===

「不知道，不过回分号一查便知。」

「接下来我想去一趟中央车站，您若不介意同行，」弗伦奇声言，「就在那边查个清楚。不过动身之前，还得请您告诉我，这些照片里可有范德肯普先生？」他指着壁炉台和墙上张挂的几张合影。

碰巧那几张合影里，正有一张带着这位失踪的外勤，而且斯霍夫斯先生和邦迪克斯夫人异口同声，作证说照得极像。

「那么这张我带走了。」弗伦奇说着，把相片揣进了口袋。

两人随后去了中央车站，查阅车次。一查才知，那班白天联运船车，要到晚上 10:05 才抵达维多利亚（Victoria）车站。此中意味，弗伦奇一点便透。据奥查德（Orchard）所言，他晚上 10:15 到达哈顿花园的公司，而且不可能更晚——这一点由奥尔康巡警（Constable Alcorn）的证词坐实。其时尸身已然冰冷，可见罪案必定发生在相当早之前。因此，一个从阿姆斯特丹搭白天班船过海的人，无论如何来不及行凶。范德肯普对斯霍夫斯说自己走的是白天班船，莫非是存心撒谎？倘是如此，可是为了做一份不在场的凭据？案发当晚早些时候，他可曾与格辛另有密约，才提前一夜渡海前去赴约？弗伦奇觉得，这些问题都非有个满意的答案不可，他在心里记下一笔：找不着答案，誓不罢休。

===Original===

With his new friend’s aid he began to interrogate the staff of the Central Station, in the hope of ascertaining whether or not the missing man had actually left by the train in question. But of this he could learn nothing. None of the employees appeared to know Vanderkemp’s appearance, nor after that lapse of time could anyone recall having seen a passenger of his description.

That day and the next French spent in the charming old city, trying to learn what he could of the missing man’s life and habits. He came across a number of persons who were acquainted with the traveller, but no one with whom he had been really intimate. None of these people could give him much information, nor did any of them seem to care whether or no Vanderkemp should ever be heard of again. From all he heard, French concluded that Vanderkemp’s character was such as might be expected in the guilty man, but there was but little evidence of motive, and none at all of guilt.

He returned to London by the night service, and having ascertained that the steamer he crossed by was the same that had run on the date of Vanderkemp’s assumed journey, he made exhaustive inquiries as to the latter from the staff on board, unfortunately with negative results.

Next day his efforts were equally fruitless. He spent most of it in discussing the situation with Mr. Duke, and trying to make a list of the persons who could have had access to the typewriter, but nowhere could he get a gleam of light. The authorship of the letter remained as inscrutable a mystery as the murder of Gething.

Having circulated a description of Vanderkemp containing a copy of the photograph, French went home that night a worried and disconsolate man. But though he did not know it, further news was even at the moment on the way to him.

===Chinese===

在这位新交的襄助下，他着手查问中央车站的员工，想弄清那位失踪者究竟有没有搭乘所说的那班火车。可这一头毫无所获。站上的雇员似乎没有一个认得范德肯普的相貌，事隔多日，也再没有人想得起曾见过如此这般的旅客。

那一天连着次日，弗伦奇都消磨在这座迷人的古城里，尽力打听失踪者的生平与习性。他遇见不少与这位外勤相识的人，却没有一个同他真正亲近的。这些人都提供不出多少情形，而且看来谁也不在乎范德肯普此后还有没有音讯。凭耳闻的一切，弗伦奇断定：论品性，范德肯普正像凶犯该有的模样；只是动机方面的凭据寥寥，罪行方面更是一丝也无。

他仍搭夜班航船回了伦敦。上船后查明，自己乘坐的这一班，与范德肯普假定行程那天的班次正是同一艘船，于是便就那天情形向船上员工细细查问，可惜一无所获。

第二天，他的奔走同样劳而无功。大半天工夫，都花在同杜克先生参详局势、开列可能接近那台打字机的人员名单上，却哪儿也寻不出一线光亮。那封信出自谁手，依旧同格辛遇害案一样，是个深不可测的谜。

把附有照片翻印件的范德肯普相貌说明分发出去之后，弗伦奇当夜才闷闷归家，满腹心事。只是他自己并不知道，就在此刻，新的消息已然在朝他送来的路上了。

## XIII Mrs. French Takes a Notion / 十三、弗伦奇太太灵机一动

===Original===
By the time Inspector French had finished supper and lit up a pipe of the special mixture he affected, he felt in considerably better form. He determined that instead of going early to bed, as he had intended while in the train, he would try to induce the long-suffering Mrs. French to listen to a statement of his problem, in the hope that light thereon would be vouchsafed to her, in which in due course he would participate.
Accordingly, when she had finished with the supper things he begged her to come and share his difficulties, and when she had taken her place in her accustomed armchair and had commenced her placid knitting, he took up the tale of his woes.
Slowly and in the fullest detail he told her all he had done from the time he was sent to Messrs. Williams & Davies, when he first heard of the mysterious Mrs. X, up to his series of visits of that day, concluding by expressing his belief that Mrs. X and Mrs. Ward were one and the same person, and explaining the difficulty he found himself up against in tracing her. She heard him without comment, and when he had finished asked what he proposed to do next.
“Why, that’s just it,” he exclaimed a trifle impatiently. “That’s the whole thing. If I was clear about that there would be no difficulty. What would you advise?”
She shook her head, and bending forward seemed to concentrate her whole attention on her knitting. This, French knew, did not indicate lack of interest in his story. It was just her way. He therefore waited more or less hopefully, and when after a few minutes she began to question him, his hopes were strengthened.

===Chinese===
弗伦奇探长吃罢晚饭，又点上一斗他素来偏爱的特制烟丝，自觉精神好了许多。他原本在火车上还打算早点安歇，这会儿却拿定了主意：要劝一劝那位任劳任怨的弗伦奇太太，来听听他的难题，指望上天肯赐她一线灵光，而那份灵光，到时候自然也有他分享的一份。
于是，等她收拾完晚饭的家什，他便恳请她来分担自己的难处。待她在惯常的那把扶手椅里坐定，安安静静地动手打起毛线，他便开始诉起苦来。
他慢条斯理、原原本本，从受命前往威廉斯与戴维斯商行、初闻那位神秘的X太太说起，一直讲到当天的一连串走访，末了说出自己的见解：X太太与沃德夫人本是同一个人；接着又说明他在追查此人下落时撞上的那道难关。她不加一句评点地听着，等他讲完，只问他下一步打算怎么办。
「嗐，难就难在这里，」他略带不耐烦地叫道，「整个案子卡住的正是这一环。这一层要是弄清了，还有什么难的。你说该怎么办？」
她摇了摇头，俯身向前，仿佛把全副心思都收拢在毛线活上。弗伦奇知道，这并不是她对讲述缺乏兴趣，她素来就是这个做派。于是他多少抱着点希望等着；过了几分钟，她开口发问，他的希望便更足了。

===Original===
“You say that Mrs. Root and those steamer people thought the woman was English?”
“That’s so.”
“There were quite a lot of them thought she was English?”
“Why, yes,” French agreed. “There was Mrs. Root and the doctor and the purser and her dinner steward and at least four stewardesses. They were all quite satisfied. And the other passengers and attendants must have been satisfied too, or the thing would have been talked about. But I don’t see exactly what you’re getting at.”
Mrs. French was not to be turned aside from her catechism.
“Well, do *you* think she was English?” she persisted.

===Chinese===
「你是说，鲁特夫人和那些邮船上的人，都当那女人是英国人？」
「是啊。」
「当她是英国人的，人数还不少？」
「呃，是啊。」弗伦奇表示同意。「有鲁特夫人，有船上的医生、事务长、伺候她用餐的侍者，还有至少四个女乘务员，个个深信不疑。其余的乘客和侍应人员想必也都信了，否则这事早就被人当闲话讲了。只是我不大明白，你这话是往哪里引。」
弗伦奇太太却是不肯让她的盘问被打岔引开。
「那么，你自己觉得她是英国人么？」她追问。

===Original===
French hesitated. Did he? He really was not sure. The evidence seemed strong, and yet it was just as strong, or stronger, for her being an American. Mr. Williams, for example, was﻿—
“You don’t know,” Mrs. French broke in. “Well, now, see here. Mr. Williams said she was American?”
“That’s it,” her husband rejoined. “He said﻿—”
“And that bank manager and his clerk, they thought she was American?”
“Yes, but﻿—”
“And the shops she bought and sold the jewellery at, and the Savoy, and the Southampton police, they all thought she was American?”

===Chinese===
弗伦奇踌躇了。他觉得么？他自己也拿不准。证据看似很硬，可要说她是美国人，证据也一样硬，甚至更硬。比方说，威廉斯先生就——
「你是拿不准。」弗伦奇太太打断他。「喏，你听着。威廉斯先生说她是个美国人，对不对？」
「正是这么回事。」她丈夫应道。「他说——」
「还有那位银行经理和他的职员，他们也当她是美国人？」
「是的，可是——」
「还有她买卖珠宝的那几家铺子，还有萨伏伊饭店、南安普敦的警察，他们全当她是美国人？」

===Original===
“Yes, but we don’t﻿—”
“Well, that ought surely to give you something.”
“That they were sisters? I thought of that, but the handwriting shows that they weren’t.”
“Of course I don’t mean sisters. Think again.”
French sat up sharply.
“What do you mean, Emily? I don’t follow what you’re after.”

===Chinese===
「是的，可我们并不能——」
「喏，这总该能给你点什么了吧。」
「说她们是姐妹俩？这个我想过，可那笔迹表明她们不是。」
「我当然不是说姐妹。再想想。」
弗伦奇腾地坐直了身子。
「你这是什么意思，埃米莉？我摸不清你到底在往哪儿想。」

===Original===
His wife ignored the interruption.
“And there’s another thing you might have thought of,” she continued. “That Williams man thought he had seen the woman before. What age is he?”
French was becoming utterly puzzled.
“What age?” he repeated helplessly. “I don’t know. About sixty, I should think.”
“Just so,” said his wife. “And that other man, that Scarlett, he thought he had seen her before. What age is he?”
The Inspector moved nervously.

===Chinese===
他的妻子全当没听见这句打岔。
「还有一件事，你也该想到的。」她接着说。「那个威廉斯，觉得自己从前见过这女人。他多大年纪？」
弗伦奇渐渐坠入五里雾中。
「多大年纪？」他一筹莫展地重复道。「我说不上来。我琢磨着总有六十岁了吧。」
「这就对了。」他妻子说。「还有一个人，那个斯卡利特，也觉得自己从前见过她。他多大年纪？」
探长局促地挪动了一下。

===Original===
“Really, Emily,” he protested, “I wish you’d explain what you’re getting at. I don’t take your meaning in the least.”
“You would if you’d use your head,” his wife snapped. “What age is that Scarlett?”
“About the same as the other﻿—fifty-five or sixty. But what has that got to do﻿—”
“But the young fellow, that bank clerk; he didn’t remember her?”
“No, but﻿—”
“Well, there you are﻿—silly! What would a woman be who could make up like another woman, and put on an English or American talk, and be remembered by old Londoners? Why, a child could guess that, Watson!”

===Chinese===
「真是的，埃米莉，」他抗辩道，「我巴不得你说个明白，你到底想讲什么。你这层意思我一点儿也捉摸不到。」
「肯动动脑子你就懂了。」他妻子没好气道。「那个斯卡利特多大年纪？」
「跟前一位差不多——五十五还是六十。可这跟那桩事有什么相干——」
「还有那个年轻人，那个银行职员，他就不记得见过她？」
「不记得，可是——」
「这不就结了——傻瓜！一个女人，能化装成另一个女人的模样，能说一口英国腔或是美国腔，又能叫上了年纪的伦敦人一见就记得住，你说她会是什么人？嗐，这种事连小孩子也猜得着啊，华生！」

===Original===
When Mrs. French called her husband by the name of the companion of the great Holmes, it signified two things, first, that she was in what he always referred to as “a good twist,” and secondly, that she felt pleasantly superior, having seen something﻿—or thinking she had﻿—which he had missed. He was therefore always delighted when a conversation reached this stage, believing that something helpful was about to materialise.
But on this occasion he grasped her meaning as soon as she had spoken. Of course! How in all the earthly world had he missed the point? The woman was an actress; a former London actress! That would explain the whole thing. And if so, he would soon find her. Actors’ club secretaries and attendants, theatrical agents, stage doorkeepers, the editors of society papers﻿—scores of people would have known her, and he would have an easy task to learn her name and her history.
He jumped up and kissed his wife. “By Jove, Emily! You’re a fair wonder,” he cried warmly, and she, still placidly knitting, unsuccessfully attempted to hide the affection and admiration she felt for him by a trite remark anent the folly of an old fool.
Next morning, French, with a new and thoroughly satisfactory programme before him, sallied forth at quite the top of his form. He had made a list of theatrical agencies at which he intended first to apply, after which, if luck had up to then eluded him, he would go round the theatres and have a word with the stage door keepers, finally applying to the older actor-managers and producers and anyone else from whom he thought he might gain information.
But his quest turned out to be even simpler than he had dared to hope. The superior young ladies of the first three agencies at which he called shook their pretty heads over the photograph and could throw no light on his problem. But at the fourth, the girl made a suggestion at which French leaped.
“No,” she said, “I don’t know anyone like that, but if she’s left the stage some time I wouldn’t; I’ve only been here about two years. And I don’t know anyone who could help you; this place has not been open very long. But I’ll tell you,” she went on, brightening up. “Mr. Rohmer is inside. If anyone in London would know, he should. If you catch him coming out you could ask him.”

===Chinese===
每当弗伦奇太太用大福尔摩斯那位搭档的名字称呼丈夫，其中便有两层意思：其一，她进入了丈夫常说的那种『来了劲儿』的状态；其二，她正自觉得意，自觉高他一筹——她看出了什么他漏掉的东西，或者自以为看出了。因此每逢谈话到了这一步，他总是满心欢喜，相信就要有点管用的东西冒出头来。
不过这一回，她话音刚落，他便抓住了她的意思。可不是么！他怎么鬼迷了心窍，竟把这层给漏了过去？那女人是个演员，是从前在伦敦登过台的女演员！这么一来，事情就全解释得通了。果真如此，他很快就能寻着她。演员俱乐部的秘书和听差、剧团经纪人、后台门房、社交报纸的编辑——认得她的人几十上百，要打听她的姓名和来历，还不是易如反掌。
他跳起身来吻了吻妻子。「天哪，埃米莉！你真是个了不起的奇人！」他热切地喊道。而她依旧安安静静打着毛线，想拿一句『老糊涂犯傻』之类的老生常谈，把心中对他的情意和倾慕掩藏过去，却掩藏得并不成功。
第二天早晨，弗伦奇揣着一份全新而又万分合意的行动计划，精神抖擞地出了门。他已开好一张剧团经纪行的单子，打算先挨家去问；倘若到那时好运还未照面，他就跑遍各家剧院，找后台门房搭话；最后再去拜访上了年纪的演员兼经理和各位制作人，以及任何他觉得能供给消息的人。
不料这一趟寻找，比他敢于指望的还要省事。他登门的前三家经纪行里，几位眼高于顶的年轻小姐对着照片摇了摇她们俏丽的脑袋，都没能给他的难题投进半点光亮。可到了第四家，那位姑娘提的一条建议，听得弗伦奇心头一跳。
「不认识。」她说。「像这模样的一个人我也不认得，不过她要是一早就离开了舞台，我自然认不得——我到这儿满打满算才两年。能帮你忙的人我也不认识，这爿店开了没多日子。不过嘛，」她接着往下说，来了精神，「罗默先生就在里头。全伦敦要有人认得，那就数得上他。你要是赶巧碰上他出来，倒可以问一问。」

===Original===
Mr. Horace Rohmer! The prince of producers! French knew his name well, though he had never met him. He thanked the girl and sat down to wait.
Presently she called to him, “He’s just going,” and French, stepping forward, saw a short, stout, rather Jewish-looking gentleman moving to the stairs. He hastened after him, and, introducing himself, produced his photograph and asked his question.
The famous producer glanced at the card and smiled.
“Oh, Lor’ yes,” he announced, “I know her. But these people wouldn’t.” He indicated the agency and its personnel with a backward nod. “She was before their time. Why, that’s the great Cissie Winter; at least, she had the makings of being great at one time. She was first lady in Panton’s company a dozen years ago or more. I remember her in *Oh, Johnny!*, *The Duchess*, *The Office Girl*, and that lot﻿—good enough plays in their day, but out of date now. I hope she’s not in trouble?”
“It’s a matter of stolen diamonds,” French answered, “but I’m not suggesting she is guilty. We want some explanations, that’s all.”
“I should be sorry to hear there was anything wrong,” Mr. Rohmer declared. “I thought a lot of her at one time, though she did go off and make a muck of things.”

===Chinese===
霍勒斯·罗默先生（Horace Rohmer）！制作人中的泰斗！弗伦奇久闻其名，只是从未会过。他谢过那姑娘，坐下等候。
不多时她招呼他：「他这就走了。」弗伦奇抢上一步，看见一位五短身材、体态肥胖、相貌颇有几分犹太人模样的绅士正朝楼梯走去。他急忙赶上，自报家门，取出照片，道明来意。
这位大名鼎鼎的制作人朝照片瞥了一眼，微微一笑。
「哦，老天爷，认得。」他声明。「我认得她。可这些人不会认得。」他朝身后点点头，指了指那家经纪行和行里的人。「她们没赶上她的年代。嘿，这位就是大名鼎鼎的西西·温特——至少她当年一度有成大器的潜质。十几年前，她是潘顿（Panton）剧团的头牌女主角。我记得她演过《哦，约翰尼！》（Oh, Johnny!）、《公爵夫人》（The Duchess）、《办公室女郎》（The Office Girl）那一路戏——搁当年都算得上好戏，如今可过时了。但愿她没惹上什么麻烦吧？」
「是一桩钻石失窃案。」弗伦奇答道。「不过我并不是说她就有嫌疑。我们要的是她作些说明，仅此而已。」
「要是听说她当真出了岔子，那我会很惋惜的。」罗默先生宣称。「我一度很看重她，尽管她后来走了岔路，把事情弄得一塌糊涂。」

===Original===
“How was that, sir?”
“Some man. Went off to live with some man, a married man, and well on to being elderly. At least, that was the story at the time. I’m not straightlaced, and I shouldn’t have minded that if she had only kept up her stage work. But she didn’t. She just dropped out of sight. And she might have risen to anything. A promising young woman lost. Sickening, I call it.”
“I suppose you could give me no hint as to how I might trace her?”
The producer shrugged his shoulders.
“Not the slightest, I’m afraid. I didn’t even know that she was alive.”
“What theatres did she play in?”

===Chinese===
「那是怎么一回事，先生？」
「为了个男人。跟一个男人走了，同居去了——一个有妇之夫，年纪也一大把了。至少当年外面是这么传的。我倒不是个古板人，只要她不撂下舞台上的营生，这种事我本不放在心上。可她偏偏撂下了。就这么从人前消失了。她本来前程未可限量。一个大有前途的年轻女演员就这么毁了。要我说，真叫人恶心。」
「您能不能给我一点指点，说说该上哪儿去找她？」
制作人耸了耸肩。
「恐怕一丝一毫也给不了。我连她还在不在人世都不知道。」
「她都在哪些剧院演过戏？」

===Original===
“Several, but it was in the Comedy she did her best work.”
“I’ll try there.”
“You can try, but don’t build too much on it. Theatrical staffs change quickly and have short memories. If you’ve no luck there you should go to Jacques﻿—you know, Richard Jacques the producer. If my memory serves me, he put out those plays I mentioned. If not, he can tell you who did.”
French was overjoyed. This was indeed a stroke of luck. He had proved his theory﻿—he was already beginning to overlook the part his wife had played in it﻿—he had done a neat piece of deduction, and it had been justified. He had now obtained information which must lead him infallibly to his goal. His next business must be at the Comedy, where, if his luck held, he might obtain information which would put him straight on the woman’s track.
As he turned away from the agency, French felt a touch on his shoulder. It was Mr. Duke, and the old gentleman greeted him warmly and asked of his progress.
“I’m just going in here for some coffee,” he went on, indicating the somewhat old-fashioned and retiring restaurant before which they stood. “Come and have a cup with me. It’s ages since I saw you or heard what you were doing.”

===Chinese===
「好几家，不过她最出彩的戏是在喜剧剧院（Comedy）演的。」
「那我就上那儿去试试。」
「试尽管去试，只是别太指望。剧院里的人手换得勤，记性也短。你在那儿要是碰不上运气，就该去找雅克——就是制作人理查德·雅克（Richard Jacques），这您知道。如果我没记错，我方才提的那几出戏正是他排演的。就算不是，他也能告诉您是谁排的。」
弗伦奇大喜过望。这真是撞上了好运。他的推断得了证实——妻子在这上头出的一份力，他已经开始淡忘了——他做了一番干净利落的推理，而且得了印证。如今他到手的情报，必然万无一失地把他引向目标。下一步就该去喜剧剧院，只要运气不撇下他，他或许能在那里得到情报，从此径直咬住那女人的踪迹。
弗伦奇刚从经纪行门口转过身，就觉出肩头被轻轻一碰。是杜克先生。老绅士热络地招呼他，问起案子的进展。
「我正要进去喝杯咖啡。」他接着说，指了指两人面前那家式样老派、并不惹眼的餐馆。「来陪我喝一杯吧。老没见着您，也不知您这些日子都在忙些什么。」

===Original===
French was full of his discovery, and eagerly seized the chance of a victim to whom to unfold the tale of his prowess. Accordingly, when they were seated in a quiet nook he began with gusto to relate his exploits. He told of his visit to Mürren, and of the photographs given to him by Mrs. Root, of his tracing the movements of the elusive lady in Southampton, of his deduction that she was an actress, and finally of his great stroke in learning her identity.
Mr. Duke, who had been following the recital with a thrilled interest that satisfied even French’s egotism, remembered the lady’s name, though he could not recall anything else about her.
“This will be good news for Vanderkemp,” he declared. “I must tell him at once. Though you have taken off your surveillance, he feels that he has never really been cleared of suspicion. This discovery of yours will go far to satisfy him. Yes, and what then?”
He settled himself again to listen, but when he realised that French had finished his tale and was no nearer finding Miss Cissie Winter than he had been of getting hold of Mrs. X, his features took on an expression of the keenest disappointment, bordering almost on despair.
“Good heavens, Inspector! After raising my hopes, don’t tell me now that you are really practically no farther on,” he lamented. Then sinking his voice, he went on slowly, “If something isn’t discovered soon I may tell you I don’t know what I’m going to do. I’m getting to the end of my tether. I’m even getting short of cash. The insurance company won’t pay﻿—yet; they say it is not certain the stones will not be recovered. They say I must wait. But my creditors won’t wait.”
He stopped and stared before him vacantly, and French, looking at him more keenly than he had yet done, was shocked to see how old and worn the man was looking. “Even if the insurance company paid all, I don’t know that I could make ends meet,” he went on presently. “I’m beginning to see ruin staring me in the face. I thought I was strong and could scoff at reverses, but I can’t, Inspector, I can’t. I’m not the man I was, and this affair has shaken me severely.”

===Chinese===
弗伦奇正满心盛着他的新发现，急切地抓住这个送上门的听众，好把自家的丰功娓娓道来。于是，两人在一个清静的角落里坐定，他便津津有味地讲起自己的功业来。他讲到米伦之行，讲到鲁特夫人送给他的那些照片，讲到他在南安普敦一路追索那位行踪飘忽的太太，讲到他推断出她是个演员，末了讲到他探明她身份的那记妙手。
杜克先生听得心驰神往，那份兴味之浓，连弗伦奇的自负都得了满足。他记得这位小姐的名字，只是旁的就想不起来了。
「这对范德肯普是个好消息。」他宣称。「我得马上告诉他。监视虽然撤了，他却总觉得自己的嫌疑从来没有真正洗干净过。您这个发现，定能让他宽慰不少。好——后来呢？」
他重新坐定，侧耳听下去；可等他明白过来，弗伦奇的故事已经到了头，而找到西西·温特小姐这一层，比起先前捉拿X太太来，不见得近了一步，他的脸上便浮起一阵深重的失望，几乎要挨着绝望的边了。
「天哪，探长！」他哀叹道，「您把我的希望都挑起来了，可别这会儿又告诉我，说您其实并没有往前挪多少。」接着他放低了声音，缓缓说道：「要是再查不出个所以然，我可以告诉您，我都不知道自己接下来该怎么办了。我已经到山穷水尽的地步了。我手头连现钱都快见底了。保险公司还不肯赔——暂时还不肯；他们说，那些钻石未必就找不回来。他们说我必须等。可我的债主们不肯等啊。」
他打住话头，失神地望着面前。弗伦奇比先前任何时候都更仔细地端详他，看到这人竟显得如此衰老憔悴，不由得心里一震。「就算保险公司如数赔了，我也不敢说就能撑住收支。」他顿了顿又说道。「我已经开始看见破产在当面瞪着我了。我原以为自己够硬朗，受得住挫折，还能对厄运一笑置之，可我不行了，探长，我不行了。我已经不是从前那个人了，这桩事把我折腾得够呛。」

===Original===
French was somewhat taken aback by this outburst, but he felt genuinely sorry for the old man, who at the close of a life of comparative luxury and success was faced with failure and poverty. He gave him what comfort he could, pointing out that the discovery of Mrs. X’s identity was a real step forward, and expressed the belief that so well known a personality could not long remain hidden.
“I sincerely trust you are right,” Mr. Duke answered, “and I am ashamed of having made such a fuss. But do try, Inspector,” he looked imploringly at the other, “do try to push on the affair. I know you are,” he smiled, “doing all that anyone could do, but it’s so desperately important to me. You understand, I hope, that I am not complaining? I fully appreciate your splendid work in the face of great difficulties.”
French assured him that he himself was just as anxious to clear up the mystery as anyone else could be, and that he need not fear but that everything possible would be done to that end, and with further expressions of mutual amity they parted.
The Inspector next turned his steps to the Comedy theatre. Rehearsals were in progress, and the building was open. Going round to the stage door, he spoke to the doorkeeper.
“No, sir,” the man said civilly, “I’m not here long. Only about nine months.”
“Who was before you?”

===Chinese===
这一番倾诉倒叫弗伦奇有些错愕，可他对老人是真心同情：大半生过得相当优裕顺遂的人，到头来却要面对破产和穷困。他好言宽慰，指出查明X太太的身份是实实在在的一大进展，并表示这样一位名人，是藏不了多久的。
「我一心指望您说得对。」杜克先生答道，「方才这么失态，我自己都臊得慌。可是务必加把劲，探长，」他恳求似的望着对方，「务必把这桩案子往前推。我知道您，」他微微一笑，「已经是人力所能做的都做了，可这对我实在是性命交关。您总明白吧，我希望，我这不是在抱怨？重重困难之下，您办得何等出色，我心里是完全领情的。」
弗伦奇向他担保，他自己同任何人一样急于解开这个谜；也请他只管放心，凡是为达此目的能做到的事，一件也不会落下。两人又彼此说了些友好的话，方才分手。
探长下一步把脚步转向喜剧剧院。院里正排着戏，楼门开着。他绕到后台门口，同门房搭了话。
「不认识，先生。」那人客气地答道，「我到这儿没多久。满打满算九个月上下。」
「您之前是谁当这份差？」

===Original===
“A man they called Dowds, an old man. He was getting too old for the job. That’s why he left.”
“Could you put me on to where I should find him?”
“I should try at the office, sir. I expect they’d have his address. To the right at the end of this passage.”
With some difficulty French found his way to the office. A young man glanced up from the desk over which he was bending. “Well, sir?” he said briskly.
French explained his business. He was inquiring as to the whereabouts of the former actress, Miss Cissie Winter, and failing information as to her, he would be obliged for the address of the ex-stagedoor keeper, Dowds, who might be able to assist him in his main inquiry.
“Miss Cissie Winter?” the sharp young man repeated. “I’ve heard of her, but she wasn’t on here in my time. Any idea of her dates or plays?”

===Chinese===
「一个人家都叫他道兹（Dowds）的，是个老头儿。他年纪太大，当不动这份差了，为这个才走的。」
「您能指点我上哪儿去找他么？」
「您该到办公室去问问，先生。我想他们准存着他的住址。这条过道走到头，靠右手就是。」
弗伦奇颇费了些周折才寻到办公室。一个年轻人从伏着的案卷上抬起头来。「什么事，先生？」他脆生生地问。
弗伦奇说明了来意。他正在查问昔日女演员西西·温特小姐的下落；关于她若是问不出消息，那么劳驾告诉他前任后台门房道兹的地址，此人或许能对他的主要查问帮上一把。
「西西·温特小姐？」那伶俐的年轻人重复了一遍。「这名字听说过，不过我在职那阵子，她早就不在这儿了。她的年头或者剧目，您有个大概么？」

===Original===
“Twelve or more years since she left the stage, I’m told. She played in *The Office Girl* and *The Duchess* and *Oh, Johnny!*”
The young man whistled beneath his breath as he sat thinking.
“ ’Fraid I can’t help you about the lady,” he declared at last. “There are no records here of twelve years back. But I can put you on to Dowds all right, or at least I can give you his address when he left us.”
“Much obliged, I’m sure.”
The young man crossed the room, and taking a book out of a cupboard, turned over the pages rapidly.
“29 Babcock Street. It’s off Charing Cross Road, about halfway down on the left hand side going south. You’ll get him there if he hasn’t moved.”

===Chinese===
「听说她离开舞台已有十二年，兴许还多。她演过《办公室女郎》《公爵夫人》，还有《哦，约翰尼！》。」
年轻人坐着寻思，唇齿间低低打了个呼哨。
「这位小姐么，怕是帮不上您的忙了。」他终于说道。「十二年前的旧档，我们这儿没有。不过道兹那儿我倒指得出门道，至少他离馆那会儿的住址，我可以给您。」
「那真是多谢了。」
年轻人穿过房间，从柜子里取出一本簿子，飞快地翻检页码。
「巴布科克街 29 号（Babcock Street）。从查令十字街插进去，往南走差不多一半路，左手一边就是。只要他没挪窝，您在那儿准找得着他。」

===Original===
French, having noted the address, turned to go.
“Wait a sec’,” said the young man. “I’m not certain, but I believe Richard Jacques put out those plays you mentioned. If so, he could probably help you better than anyone. He does business at that new place he has taken over, the Aladdin in Piccadilly. You should try him.”
French thanked his new friend, and after again traversing the endless corridors of the huge building, found himself once more in the street.
At 29 Babcock Street the door was opened to him by a respectable-looking woman, who said that her husband, Peter Dowds, was within. His health was poor, but if the gentleman would come in, he would make shift to come down to see him.
French sat down to wait in the tiny parlour. Presently a shuffling became audible in the hall, and the door, opening slowly, revealed a short but immensely stout man, whose small eyes blinked inquisitively at his visitor as the latter rose and wished him good day.
“Good day, good day,” the man wheezed, as he steered himself across the room and sank into one of the chairs. “It’s the asthma,” he went on in a husky voice. “It’s always bad this time of year.” He stopped and sat panting, then went on, “You wanted to see me?”

===Chinese===
弗伦奇记下地址，转身要走。
「您稍待。」年轻人说。「我不敢说准，不过我记得，您提到的那些戏正是理查德·雅克排演的。果真如此，他多半比谁都能帮上您。他如今就在新接手的那家场子里办事，就是皮卡迪利的阿拉丁剧院（Aladdin）。您该去寻寻他。」
弗伦奇谢过这位新朋友，重新穿过那座大楼里没完没了的走廊，又回到了街上。
在巴布科克街 29 号，替他开门的是一位看上去很体面的妇人。她说丈夫彼得·道兹（Peter Dowds）就在家里，只是身子骨不好；不过这位先生要是肯进来，他会设法下楼来见客。
弗伦奇在狭小的客厅里坐下等候。不多时，门厅里传来拖沓的脚步声，房门缓缓打开，露出一个身材矮小却异常肥胖的男人。客人起身问好，那双小眼睛便好奇地朝他眨着。
「您好，您好。」那人喘着气说，一边把身子挪过房间，沉进一把椅子里。「是气喘病。」他哑着嗓子接着说。「年年这个时节都要犯。」他停下坐着喘气，随后又接着说：「您找我有事？」

===Original===
“Yes,” French admitted, “but I’m sorry to find your asthma so bad. What do you do for it?”
The Inspector had found from long experience that the time spent in discussing his illness with an invalid was not wasted. The pleasure he gave had the effect of creating a sympathy and good feeling which assisted him when he came to the second part of the interview, the favour he wanted for himself. He was not altogether a hypocrite in this. It was part of the technique of his business, and besides, he was a good-natured man who really did like giving pleasure. He therefore talked asthma and asthma cures for some minutes before turning to the subject of Miss Cissie Winter.
But in the present case the excellent impression which he undoubtedly produced brought him but little benefit. The stout old doorkeeper remembered Miss Winter well, and instantly recognised her photograph, but he knew nothing about her present whereabouts. She had gone off with some man, a man whom also he remembered well, as on many occasions they had chatted together while the former waited at the stage door for the lady’s appearance. He was tall and well built, well on in middle age, and with the air of a professional or business man. His name, Dowds believed, was Vane, but of this he was not positive. Asked how he knew that the lady had gone off with this or any other man, it transpired that he did not really know at all, but that this had been the generally accepted theory at the time. He had never learned the man’s address, but he seemed to have plenty of money and was liberal in his tips. Since that time, about thirteen years previously, Dowds had not heard or seen anything of either. Of Miss Winter he had but a poor opinion. She might be a good actress, but she was hard and mean and had a sharp tongue. What the man could have seen in her he, Dowds, did not know, but he had evidently been pretty completely bowled over.
When French had gleaned these particulars, he found he had reached the end of the old doorkeeper’s usefulness, and he was soon on his way to his next call, the Aladdin theatre in Piccadilly.
Mr. Jacques was in the building, but engaged, and French fretted and fumed for nearly two hours before being ushered into his presence. But then he felt himself completely compensated for his long wait. Like most others who came in contact with him, French soon fell a victim to the great producer’s winning personality and charm of manner. The old gentleman apologised courteously for his engagement, which, he explained, was a troublesome rehearsal, and then listened with close attention to what French had to say.

===Chinese===
「是。」弗伦奇承认道，「只是看到您这气喘病犯得这样凶，我心里很过意不去。您平时都怎么治呢？」
探长凭多年的经验深知，同病人聊聊他的病，这点工夫绝不白费。他给对方的那份愉快，会生出一种投契的好感，等谈到会面的下半截——他自己要请托的事——便大有助益。在这上头，他也不全然是个伪君子。这是他这一行的技巧之一，何况他心地厚道，本来就喜欢给人愉快。因此他先就气喘病和气喘病的疗法谈了好几分钟，才把话头转到西西·温特小姐身上。
然而这一次，他无疑给人留下的极佳印象却没带来多大好处。肥胖的老门房对温特小姐记得清清楚楚，一眼就认出了她的照片，可她如今身在何处，他却一无所知。她是跟一个男人走的，那男人他也记得真切，因为早先好多次，那男人在后台门口等小姐出场的时候，两个人曾攀谈过。他身材高大，体格健壮，已到中年后期，带着专业人士或生意人的气派。那人的名字，道兹记得好像是文恩，只是这一点他没有把握。问到他是怎么知道小姐跟这个或别的男人走的，原来他压根儿并不知情，只是当年大家普遍都这么讲罢了。那男人的住址他从未得知，只知他似乎很有钱，赏起小费来出手大方。打那时起——约莫十三年前——这两个人道兹就再没听见过、看见过。说起温特小姐，他的评价不高。戏也许演得好，可人心肠又硬又小气，嘴上还不饶人。那男人到底看中了她什么，他道兹不知道，不过瞧那情形，他显然是被迷得七荤八素了。
这些底细搜罗到手，弗伦奇发觉老门房的用处也就到了头，不多时他便动身去下一处——皮卡迪利的阿拉丁剧院。
雅克先生人在楼里，只是脱不开身，弗伦奇急得团团转，等了将近两个钟头才被领了进去。不过随即他便觉得，这番久候完全得到了补偿。同大多数接触过他的人一样，弗伦奇很快也被这位大制作人动人的品格和翩翩的风度折服了。老绅士为方才的失陪殷勤致歉，解释说那是场麻烦的排练，接着便聚精会神地听弗伦奇讲述来意。

===Original===
But he could not tell so very much after all. He remembered Miss Winter, and after a search through some old records was able to give some details of her life. He had first seen her in the Tivoli theatre in New York, some sixteen years previously, and had been struck by her acting. She had somehow learned of his presence, for she had followed him to his hotel, and explaining that she was anxious to get a footing on the English stage, had asked him for a part in one of the plays she had heard he was then bringing out. He had agreed, and when she had completed her New York engagement, she had followed him to England, and he had starred her in *Oh, Johnny!* and certain other plays of that period. In all she had appeared in seven productions, and Mr. Jacques had a high opinion of her capabilities.
Some three years later she had given him notice that she wished to leave the stage at the end of her then current contract. He had protested, telling her that she was ruining an extremely promising career, but she had insisted, explaining that she was going to be married. This he had not believed, though he had no definite reason for his opinion. It was generally accepted that she had gone off with some married man, but how this story arose he could not say. He had, at all events, completely lost sight of her. Her age when she left his company thirteen years earlier was twenty-nine, and her address was 17 Stanford Street, Chelsea.
“I’m afraid,” French said, “that she has turned crook,” and he outlined her impersonation of Mrs. Root.
“Of course I know nothing about that,” Mr. Jacques answered, “but I can at least tell you that no one could have carried out a scheme of the kind better than Cissie Winter. She had the brains and the nerve and the knowledge. I’m sorry to hear she has gone wrong, but if you are up against her, I can assure you you’ll find her no mean antagonist.”
French smiled ruefully as he rose.

===Chinese===
不过说到底，他也讲不出太多。他记得温特小姐，翻检了一番旧档案后，还能说出她生平的一些细节。约莫十六年前，他初次在纽约的蒂沃利剧院（Tivoli）见到她，她的演技叫他为之侧目。她不知从哪儿得知他到了纽约，竟一路跟到旅馆，说明自己一心想在英国舞台上立足，求他在一出戏里给她个角色——她听说他当时正要推出那出戏。他应允了。等她在纽约的演出合同期满，她便随他来到英国，他让她在《哦，约翰尼！》以及那个时期的另外几出戏里挂头牌。她前后出演了七部戏，雅克先生对她的才力评价很高。
约莫三年之后，她向他提出，想在当时那份合同期满后告别舞台。他极力劝阻，说她这是在亲手毁掉一段前程似锦的演艺生涯，她却执意如此，只说自己要结婚了。这话他不信，虽说也讲不出确切的缘由。大家当时都认定她是跟某个有妇之夫走的，可这说法是怎么起来的，他就说不上了。无论如何，他是彻底失了她的音讯。十三年前她离开他的剧团时，年方二十九岁，住址是切尔西（Chelsea）的斯坦福街 17 号（Stanford Street）。
「恐怕，」弗伦奇说，「她已经走上了邪路。」接着他把那女人冒充鲁特夫人的勾当略述了一遍。
「这个我自然一概不知。」雅克先生答道。「不过我至少可以告诉您：这一路勾当，再没有谁比西西·温特干得更利落的了。她有头脑，有胆量，有门道。听说她走了歪道，我心里很难受；不过您要对付的既然是她，我可以向您担保，她绝不是一个好对付的对手。」
弗伦奇起身时苦笑了一下。

===Original===
“I’ve discovered that already,” he admitted, “but knowing what I know now, it can’t be long until I have my hands on her.”
“I suppose I ought to wish you luck,” Mr. Jacques declared, holding out his hand, “but I don’t know that I can. I thought a lot of the young woman once, and I’m sorry that she’s in trouble.”
Inspector French, having cabled to the New York police asking for information as to the actress’s early history, made his way to 17 Stanford Street, which he found was a better-class boarding house. But here he could learn nothing. The former proprietor was dead, and none of the present staff had been connected with the place for thirteen years, or had ever heard of Miss Winter.
Disappointed once more, he returned to the Yard and put through his earlier scheme. He arranged to have the lady’s photograph inserted in the next number of the *Police Bulletin*, together with the best description of her that he could write, and a note that she was wanted. It was not a promising clue, but it was all he had left.

===Chinese===
「这一点我已经领教过了。」他承认道，「不过如今我既知道了她的底细，要不了多久，我就能把她抓到手。」
「照理我该祝您马到成功。」雅克先生伸出手来，说道，「可我不知道这祝词念不念得出口。我一度很看重那个姑娘，她落到这步田地，我实在难过。」
弗伦奇探长给纽约警方拍去电报，请他们提供这位女演员早年经历的资料，随后便前往斯坦福街 17 号，发现那是一家上等寄宿公寓。可在这里他一无所获。从前的店主已经过世，如今的人手没有一个在这家公寓里做满十三年，也从没人听说过温特小姐。
再度失望之余，他回到苏格兰场，把先前拟好的方案付诸实行。他安排把那位太太的照片登在下一期《警务公报》（Police Bulletin）上，配上他所能写出的最详尽的相貌描述，并注明此女正被通缉。这条线索并不教人看好，可他手里也只剩下这一张牌了。

## IX Mrs. Root of Pittsburg / 九、匹兹堡的鲁特夫人

===Original===

One morning about six weeks after the murder in Hatton Garden, Inspector French was summoned to the presence of his chief.

“Look here, French,” he was greeted, “you’ve been at that Gething case long enough. I can’t have any more time wasted on it. What are you doing now?”

French, his usual cheery confidence sadly deflated, hesitatingly admitted that at the moment he was not doing very much, embellishing this in the course of a somewhat painful conversation with the further information that he was doing nothing whatever, and that he was severally up against it and down and out.

“I thought so,” the chief declared. “In that case you’ll have time to go and see Williams & Davies, of Cockspur Street, the moneylenders. I have just had a phone from them, and they say that some diamonds recently came into their possession which they are told resemble those stolen from Duke & Peabody. You might look into the matter.”

===Chinese===

哈顿花园（Hatton Garden）凶案过后约六个星期，一天上午，弗伦奇探长被召到上司跟前。

「我说，弗伦奇，」上司劈头便道，「格辛那桩案子，你办得也够久的了。我可不能再让工夫白白耗在这上头。你眼下都在做些什么？」

弗伦奇平日那副乐呵呵的自信此刻瘪了大半，他吞吞吐吐地承认，眼下手头做得不多；在一场颇为难堪的谈话里，又不得不把这话说得更透：他压根儿什么也没做，而且已经走投无路、一败涂地。

「我料到是这样。」上司断言。「既然如此，你有工夫去见见科克斯珀街（Cockspur Street）那家放贷商行威廉斯与戴维斯（Williams & Davies）了。我刚接到他们的电话，说是近日有一批钻石落到他们手里，据他们所闻，同杜克与皮博迪公司（Duke & Peabody）被窃的那批颇为相似。这事你去查一查。」

===Original===

It was a rejuvenated French that fifteen minutes later ascended the stairs of Straker House, Cockspur Street, to the office of Messrs. Williams & Davies. Gone was the lassitude and the dejection and the weary brooding look, and instead there was once again the old cheery optimism, the smiling self-confidence, the springy step. He pushed open a swing door, and with an air of fatherly benevolence demanded of a diminutive office boy if Mr. Williams was in.

The senior partner was disengaged, and two minutes later French was ushered into a small, rather dark office, in which sat a tall, well-groomed man with graying hair, and a precise, somewhat pedantic manner.

“They phoned me from the Yard that you were coming across, Inspector,” he announced, when French had introduced himself. “I can only say I hope I have not brought you on a wild goose chase. But the affair should certainly be looked into.”

“I have not heard the circumstances yet, sir,” French reminded him. “I shall naturally be glad if you can give me some helpful information.”

===Chinese===

一刻钟后，登上科克斯珀街斯特雷克大厦（Straker House）的楼梯、走向威廉斯与戴维斯商行办公室的，已是一个焕然一新的弗伦奇。倦怠、消沉和那副苦苦盘算的倦容一扫而空，取而代之的又是旧日那股乐天的劲头、含笑的自信和轻快有弹性的步子。他推开一扇弹簧门，带着一副慈父般的和气，向一个矮小的办公室听差打听威廉斯先生在不在。

资深合伙人正得空，两分钟后，弗伦奇便被让进一间小而相当昏暗的办公室，里面坐着一位高个子男人，头发花白，修饰整洁，举止一丝不苟，还带着几分学究气。

「苏格兰场来电话，说您要过来，探长。」待弗伦奇通报了来意，他说道。「我只能说，但愿我没有白白劳您跑这一趟。不过这件事确实应当查一查。」

「事情的经过我还没听您讲呢，先生。」弗伦奇提醒他。「您若能供给我些有帮助的情况，我自然求之不得。」

===Original===

“I did not care to give details over the phone,” Mr. Williams explained. “You can never tell who overhears you. I once heard a girl declining what was evidently a proposal of marriage. The circumstances in this case are very simple. About six weeks ago a lady, giving her name as Mrs. Chauncey S. Root, and evidently an American, called and asked if she could see one of the principals of my firm. She was shown in to me, and she explained that she was the wife of a Mr. Chauncey S. Root, a rich steel manufacturer of Pittsburg. She had just crossed by the *Olympic* for a holiday in Europe, reaching London on the previous evening. She said a series of misfortunes had brought her into a somewhat awkward predicament, and she wondered if I could do anything to assist her. In the first place she had been foolish enough to get into a gambling set on the way over, and had lost, as she expressed it, ‘the hell of a lot of money.’ She spoke in a very racy and American way, but she gave me the impression of being thoroughly competent and efficient. Her losses ran into several hundred pounds﻿—she did not tell me the exact amount﻿—but all her ready money was gone and in addition she had given several I.O.U.s. This, however, she would not have thought twice about, as she had letters of credit for many times the amount, had it not been that a further calamity befell her in Southampton. There, in the crush on the quays, the small despatch case in which she kept her ready money and papers had been snatched from her, and she was left practically penniless, as well as without her letters of credit and her passport or other means of identification. She had, of course, reported the matter to the police authorities, but they had rather shaken their heads over it, though promising to do everything possible. She had had, indeed, to borrow a twenty-pound note from one of her travelling acquaintances to get her to London, and now she was practically without money at all. She wished, therefore, to borrow £3,000, which would enable her to pay her gambling debts and to carry on in London until fresh letters of credit could be sent. Fortunately, she had with her a collection of unmounted diamonds, which she intended to have set by London jewellers, of whose skill she had heard great accounts. These diamonds she proposed to deposit as security, and she would agree to pay whatever rate of interest was customary. She asked me if my firm would be prepared to lend the money on these terms.”

“Why did she not cable to her husband?”

“I asked her that, and she explained that she did not wish to tell Mr. Root, as he had an inveterate dislike to gambling, and they had had several disagreements about her betting proclivities. In fact, relations had been seriously strained until she had promised amendment, and a confession might easily lead to a serious breach. She could not, either, attribute the loss to the theft, as it ran to so great a figure that she could not possibly be carrying the amount in her despatch case. She said she would prefer to borrow the money until she could write to her man of business to realise some of her own stocks.

===Chinese===

「电话里我不便细说。」威廉斯先生解释道。「隔墙有耳，谁知道谁在偷听。有一回我就听见一位姑娘在回绝人家，听那口气分明是一桩求婚。本案的情节很简单。大约六个星期前，有位太太上门，自报姓名是昌西·S·鲁特夫人（Mrs. Chauncey S. Root），一看便知是位美国人，问能不能见敝行的一位主管。她被领到我跟前，说明她是昌西·S·鲁特先生的太太，鲁特先生是匹兹堡（Pittsburg）一位富有的钢铁制造商。她刚乘*奥林匹克号*（Olympic）邮船渡海来欧洲度假，前一夜抵达伦敦。她说一连串的灾祸把她弄进了相当为难的境地，不知我能不能想办法帮她一把。头一件，她在横渡的航程中糊涂到掺进了一伙赌客，用她自己的话说，输掉了『多得要命的一大笔钱』。她谈吐生动泼辣，满口美国腔，可给我的印象是，这个人十分干练，办事有章有法。她的输账累计有几百镑——确切数目她没有说——现钱已荡然无存，另外还立了好几张借据。不过这原也犯不着多想，因为她名下的信用证比这个数目大出许多倍；坏就坏在南安普敦（Southampton）又遭了一劫。在那里的码头上，人群拥挤之中，她那只存放现款和证件的小公文箱被人一把夺去，弄得她几乎分文皆无，信用证、护照以及其他一切证明身份的凭据也一并失去。这事儿她自然呈报了警方，可警方大抵只是摇头，虽说答应尽力而为。事实上，她还是向一位旅伴借了一张二十镑的纸币，才得以到伦敦，眼下简直不名一文。因此她想借 3,000 镑，好清偿赌债，并在新的信用证寄到之前在伦敦维持开销。所幸她随身带着一批未镶嵌的钻石，原打算交伦敦的珠宝匠镶嵌——伦敦同行的手艺，她早听人夸了个遍。她提出拿这批钻石作押，利息几何，一律照惯例给付。她问我，敝行可愿照这些条件放出这笔款。」

「她为什么不给她丈夫打个电报？」

「我也这样问她。她解释说，她不愿让鲁特先生知道，因为他对赌博深恶痛绝，为她好赌的脾性，两口子已经拌过好几回嘴。事实上，直到她答应悔改，夫妻关系一直紧张得很，这一番招认，只怕轻而易举就闹出一场大决裂。这笔亏空她又不能推到失窃上头，因为数额太大，说她把这样一笔款子装在小公文箱里随身带着，是怎么也讲不通的。她说，宁可先借这笔钱，等写信吩咐她的经纪人变卖一些她本人名下的股票，再作道理。

===Original===

“I said that her proposition, as such, was acceptable, as we frequently took stones and jewellery as security for loans, but that as she was a stranger to us, before we could do business we should obviously require some evidence of her bona fides. She replied that that was all right, that she quite recognised that owing to the loss of her papers and particularly of her passport something of the kind would be necessary. She said we could make what inquiries we liked, provided only we were quick about them, for she wanted the money as soon as possible. She asked how long we should take, and when I said twenty-four hours, she admitted that was reasonable. She suggested that if we did business we should take the stones to be valued to one of the best-known London jewellers. I agreed to this, and rang up Mr. Stronge, of Hurst & Stronge, of Bond Street, to ask him if he would undertake the valuation. He is, as you probably know, one of the most famous experts in the world. He consented, and I settled with him the amount of his fee. Finally it was arranged that, provided our inquiries were satisfactory, I should meet Mrs. Root at Hurst & Stronge’s at half-past ten on the following morning, she with the stones and I with my cheque book. I was to pay her five-sixths of the value of the diamonds. She said she expected to pay back the loan in about four weeks, and suitable terms of interest were arranged.”

Mr. Williams paused and glanced at his companion, as though to assure himself that his story was receiving the attention he evidently felt it deserved. But French’s air of thrilled interest left him no room for doubt, and he continued:

“I made my inquiries, and all appeared satisfactory. I called up Mrs. Root at the Savoy, told her I was prepared to deal, and at the hour named met her at Hurst & Stronge’s. Mr. Stronge took us to his private room, and there Mrs. Root produced a bag of stones, mostly diamonds, though there were a few emeralds and a large ruby, all unmounted. There were sixteen stones ranging in value from £40 to £400, but averaging about £200 or £220. Mr. Stronge valued them very carefully, and after a long wait we got his opinion. The whole were worth about £3,300, and in accordance with our bargain I proposed to hand Mrs. Root a cheque for £2,750. She admitted the correctness of this, but said she wanted the £3,000, and after some conversation I agreed to meet her wishes and filled the cheque for the latter sum. She then objected that no bank would pay her without inquiring as to her identity, which would mean another delay, and asked me if I would go with her to the bank to certify that she was the person for whom I intended the money. I agreed to this, and we went to the Piccadilly branch of the London and Counties Bank. There we saw the manager, and there I left her. I returned here and lodged the stones in my safe.”

===Chinese===

「我说，她这个提议本身可以接受，宝石首饰押款，本行素来常做；只是她于我们是生客，生意要做成，显然总得先有些凭证，证明她诚信无欺。她答说那不成问题，她完全明白，由于证件失窃、尤其是护照丢了，这一类的举措在所难免。她说我们尽管去查，想查什么都随我们，只求手脚快些，因为她急着用钱。她问我们需要多少时候，我说二十四小时，她承认这还算公道。她提议，若交易做成，该把宝石送到伦敦最有名的珠宝行之一去估价。我应承下来，便打电话给邦德街（Bond Street）赫斯特与斯特朗商行（Hurst & Stronge）的斯特朗先生（Mr. Stronge），问他可愿承办这桩估价。您大概也知道，他是世上最有名的专家之一。他应允了，酬金的数目也由我们商定。最后讲妥：只要我们查询的结果满意，次日上午十点半，我同鲁特夫人在赫斯特与斯特朗商行碰头，她携宝石，我带支票簿。我照钻石价值的六分之五付她。她说预计四个星期左右归还借款，相宜的利息条件也一并讲定。」

威廉斯先生顿了顿，望了望对方，像是要确认他这番叙述正受到他显然认为应得的关注。弗伦奇那副听得入迷的神气不容他起半点疑心，于是他接着往下讲：

「我作了查询，一切似乎满意。我打电话到萨伏伊饭店（Savoy）知会鲁特夫人，说我愿意成交，到了约定的钟点，便在赫斯特与斯特朗商行同她见了面。斯特朗先生把我们领进他的私室，鲁特夫人当场取出一袋宝石，主要是钻石，也有几颗祖母绿和一颗大的红宝石，全都未曾镶嵌。一共十六颗，价值从 40 镑到 400 镑不等，平均约在 200 镑至 220 镑之间。斯特朗先生估得极其仔细，等了好半天，我们才听到他的意见。全部合计约值 3,300 镑，依照我们的约定，我提出付鲁特夫人一张 2,750 镑的支票。她承认数目不差，却说她要的是 3,000 镑；又谈了一阵，我答应依她，便照后一个数目填了支票。随后她又提出异议，说银行不问明她的身份就不肯付款给她，那便又要耽搁一场，问我可愿陪她同去银行，证明她就是我指定付款的那个人。我答应了，我们便同去伦敦郡银行（London and Counties Bank）皮卡迪利（Piccadilly）分行。我们在那里见了经理，我把她留在那里，自己回了商行，把宝石锁进我的保险柜。」

===Original===

“The manager took your identification, I suppose?”

“Oh, yes. I know him personally and there was no difficulty. That ended the matter as far as I was concerned, and for four weeks I thought no more of it. But as the fifth and sixth week passed and the lady made no sign, I began to wonder. I telephoned to the Savoy, but it appeared she had left on the day of our deal. I assumed, however, that she was on the Continent, and no suspicion that all was not right occurred to me.”

“Then what roused your suspicion?”

“I am coming to that,” Mr. Williams answered in a slightly frigid tone. “This morning I happened to show the stones﻿—without saying how they came into my possession, of course﻿—to a personal friend of my own, a diamond merchant named Sproule, who had called with me on other business. When he saw them he grew very much excited, and asked me where I had got them from. I pressed him for an explanation, and he said they fitted the description circulated of those stolen from Messrs. Duke & Peabody. He was emphatic that I should inform the firm, but I thought it better to ring you up instead.”

“Very wise, sir,” French approved. “That was certainly your proper course. Now, I take it the first thing we have to do is to see if your friend, Mr. Sproule, is correct in his supposition. I have a list of the missing stones in my pocket, but I don’t know that I’m expert enough to identify them. I think we’ll have Mr. Duke over. May I use your phone?”

===Chinese===

「那位经理想必收下了您替她作的身份担保吧？」

「哦，是的。我同他本人相熟，毫无周折。就我而论，事情到此为止，往后四个星期，我再没把它放在心上。可是第五、第六个星期过去，那位太太毫无音讯，我便犯起疑来。我打电话到萨伏伊饭店，得到的答复却是：就在我们成交的当天，她已经离了店。不过我当时只当她去了欧陆，一点没疑心事情有什么不对。」

「那么，是什么引起了您的怀疑？」

「这就讲到正题了。」威廉斯先生的语气微微一冷。「今天早上，我碰巧把那些宝石给一位私交看——自然没有说它们是怎么到我手里的——他是位钻石商，名叫斯普劳尔（Sproule），来商行是为着别的事。他一见这些宝石就激动得了不得，问我从哪里弄来的。我追问缘故，他说这些宝石同通报传发的杜克与皮博迪公司失窃钻石的描述正相吻合。他力劝我务必知会那家商行，可我想，还是先打电话给你们为妙。」

「十分明智，先生。」弗伦奇称许道。「那确乎是您的正办。依我看，眼下第一件要办的，是查明您这位朋友斯普劳尔先生的猜想是否属实。我口袋里有一张失窃宝石的清单，只是不敢说我的眼力够得上辨认它们。我想我们该请杜克先生过来一趟。可以借用您的电话吗？」

===Original===

Mr. Duke was naturally eager to learn details of the new development, and in less than half an hour he joined the others in Mr. Williams’s office. French explained the situation, ending up, “Now we want you, Mr. Duke, to tell us if these were among the stones you lost.”

The diamond merchant, obviously much excited, began at once to make his examination. He inspected the stones minutely through a lens, weighed them on a delicate balance he had brought, and put them to other tests which greatly interested his companions. As he put each down he gave his judgment. One after another were identified. All were among those stolen from him. They were the sixteen smallest and least valuable stones of the collection.

The fact was learned by the three men with very different emotions. Mr. Duke’s gain was Mr. Williams’s loss, and resulting satisfaction and consternation showed on their respective faces, while French’s countenance wore an expression of the liveliest delight, not unmixed with mystification.

“Good heavens!” Mr. Williams cried, his voice trembling with agitation and excitement. “Then I’ve been swindled! Swindled out of three thousand pounds!” He glared at the Inspector as if he were at fault. “I suppose,” he continued, “that if this gentleman establishes his claim, the loss will fall on me? God knows, I can ill afford it.”

===Chinese===

杜克先生自然急于得知这一新进展的详情，不到半个钟头，便到威廉斯先生的办公室同他们会齐了。弗伦奇把情形说明白，末了道：「现在就要请您看看，杜克先生，这些宝石在不在您失窃的那批之列。」

那位钻石商人显然兴奋得很，立刻动手查验。他隔着放大镜逐颗细察，用随身带来的一架精密天平称量，还做了些旁的试验，看得两位同伴趣味盎然。每放下一颗，他便报出判断。宝石一颗接一颗地认了出来——全都在他失窃的那批之中，正是那批收藏里最小、最不值钱的十六颗。

三个人怀着截然不同的心情得悉了这个事实。杜克先生之所得，即威廉斯先生之所失，两张脸上各自现出满意与惊惶，而弗伦奇的神色则是喜形于色，其中又搅着几分迷惑。

「天哪！」威廉斯先生喊道，声音激动兴奋得发颤。「这么说，我是被骗了！被骗去了三千镑啊！」他瞪着探长，倒像错在探长似的。「我猜，」他接着说，「要是这位先生确立了他的权利，损失就落到我头上了？老天知道，这笔损失我可担当不起。」

===Original===

“We shall hope not, sir,” French said sympathetically. “We shall hope that with luck you’ll recover your money. But we must not waste any more time. I shall start by going to the bank to see if all the money has been withdrawn. I’d be obliged, Mr. Williams, if you would come also. I’ll keep you advised, Mr. Duke, how things go on, and of course you’ll get back your stones after the usual formalities have been carried out.”

Mr. Williams had recovered his composure, and, the gems having been locked in his safe, the three men left the office and descended to the street. There French said good day to Mr. Duke, who somewhat reluctantly took his leave, the other two continuing to the bank. After a few moments’ wait they were shown into the manager’s room.

“I am afraid, Mr. Scarlett, I have had a serious misfortune,” Mr. Williams began, almost before they were seated. “I have just learned that I have been swindled out of £3,000. This is Inspector French of Scotland Yard, and we both want your help in the matter.”

Mr. Scarlett, a well-groomed, middle-aged gentleman of fashionable appearance and suave manners, looked suitably concerned. He shook hands with French, and expressed his commiseration with his client’s loss in a few easy words, declaring also his desire to be of service.

===Chinese===

「我们盼着不至于，先生。」弗伦奇同情地说。「我们盼着您运气好，能把钱追回来。但我们不能再耽搁了。我头一步就去银行，看看那笔钱是不是已经全部提出。威廉斯先生，想劳您也走一趟。杜克先生，事情怎么进展，我会随时奉告；当然，例行手续办完之后，宝石自会发还给您。」

威廉斯先生已恢复了镇定。宝石锁进了他的保险柜，三人便离开办公室，下楼走到街上。弗伦奇在街上同杜克先生道了别——杜克先生多少有些恋恋不舍地告辞而去——另外两人继续往银行去。稍等了片刻，他们就被让进经理室。

「只怕我是遭了一场大祸，斯卡利特先生。」两人几乎还没落座，威廉斯先生便开了口。「我刚刚得知，自己被骗去了 3,000 镑。这位是苏格兰场的弗伦奇探长，我们俩都要请您在这件事上帮忙。」

斯卡利特先生（Mr. Scarlett）是位衣饰讲究的中年绅士，气派时髦，态度圆融，闻言露出恰如其分的关切。他同弗伦奇握了手，用几句熨帖的话对这位客户遭损表示慰问，并表明乐于效劳。

===Original===

“Do you remember,” Mr. Williams went on eagerly, “my coming to see you one morning about six weeks ago with a lady whom I introduced as Mrs. Root, of Pittsburg, U.S.A.? She held my cheque for £3,000, and I came to introduce her to you.”

The manager recalled the incident.

“That money was a loan, for which she deposited with me a number of diamonds. The diamonds were valued by Mr. Stronge of Hurst & Stronge’s, and I gave her less than their value. I thought I had taken all reasonable precautions, but now,” Mr. Williams made a faint gesture of despair, “now it seems that they were stolen.”

“Stolen?” Mr. Scarlett repeated in a shocked voice. “My dear sir! Allow me to say how extremely sorry I am to have to tell you that I fear your discovery has come too late. Your cheque had been paid practically in full.”

Mr. Williams gave a little groan, though he had evidently been expecting the bad news. He would have spoken, but French broke in with, “Is that so, sir? That is really what we came to ask. Now I want you please to give me as detailed an account of the whole business as you can.”

===Chinese===

「您可记得，」威廉斯先生急切地接着说，「大约六个星期前的一个上午，我领着一位太太来见您，把她介绍作美国匹兹堡的鲁特夫人？她手里拿着我开的 3,000 镑支票，我就是来把她引见给您的。」

经理记起了这件事。

「那笔钱是一笔贷款，她在我这里押下了一批钻石。钻石由赫斯特与斯特朗商行的斯特朗先生估了价，我付给她的少于估值。我自以为该有的防范都已齐备，可如今，」威廉斯先生做了个近乎绝望的手势，「如今看来，它们竟是赃物。」

「赃物？」斯卡利特先生愕然重说道。「哎呀，先生！不得不告诉您这样的话，请容我说一句，我实在万分遗憾——只怕您的发现来得太迟了。您那张支票，实际上已经如数付讫。」

威廉斯先生低低呻吟了一声，尽管这坏消息显然早在意料之中。他正要开口，弗伦奇插了进来：「当真如此吗，先生？我们要打听的正是这个。现在请您把这件事的始末，尽量详尽地讲给我听。」

===Original===

“I will do so, of course,” Mr. Scarlett returned, “but I fear my story will not help you much.” He raised his desk telephone. “Ask Mr. Plenteous to come here,” he directed, and when a young, fair-haired man had entered he resumed, “This is Mr. Plenteous who carried out the details of the transaction. As Mr. Williams has said, he and the lady called on me,” he turned over the leaves of a diary, “about midday on Thursday, 26th November. He introduced the lady as a Mrs. Chauncey S. Root, of Pittsburg, and stated he had called to certify that she was the person referred to in a cheque he had made out. She produced a cheque for £3,000, and Mr. Williams identified it as his. She thanked him and he withdrew. She then said that she wished to open a temporary account, and that she would like cash for £1,500, and to lodge the remainder. I sent for Mr. Plenteous, and asked him to arrange the matter, and he showed the lady out to his counter. Next day the balance was withdrawn except for a few shillings, which I believe we still hold. Is not that correct, Plenteous?”

“Yes, sir,” the fair-haired young man answered, “quite correct. I can turn you up the exact balance in a moment.”

“Presently, thank you, Mr. Plenteous,” French interposed. “In the meantime perhaps you would tell us what took place between you and the lady after you left this office.”

After a glance at his chief, the clerk answered:

===Chinese===

「我自然照办，」斯卡利特先生答道，「只怕我的叙述帮不了您多大的忙。」他拿起桌上的电话。「请普伦蒂厄斯先生过来一趟。」他吩咐道。等一个金发的年轻人进来后，他接着说：「这位就是经手这笔交易细节的普伦蒂厄斯先生（Mr. Plenteous）。正如威廉斯先生所说，他和那位太太来见过我，」他翻动一册日记簿的纸页，「时间是 11 月 26 日星期四，正午前后。他把那位太太介绍作美国匹兹堡的昌西·S·鲁特夫人，并说明来意，是要证明她正是他开出的一张支票上所指的人。她取出一张 3,000 镑的支票，威廉斯先生认明是他开的。她道了谢，他便退了出去。随后她说，她想开一个临时账户，要 1,500 镑现款，其余存入。我派人去请普伦蒂厄斯先生，叫他料理此事，他便领那位太太出去，到了他的柜台。第二天，余款除剩几个先令外尽数提走，那几个先令，我想如今还在我们手里。是这样吗，普伦蒂厄斯？」

「是的，先生，」金发青年答道，「分毫不差。回头我随时可以把确切的余额调出来给您看。」

「待一会儿再看，谢谢您，普伦蒂厄斯先生。」弗伦奇插言道。「这会儿，或许请您讲一讲，您离开这间办公室之后，同那位太太之间又有些什么经过。」

书记员望了上司一眼，答道：

===Original===

“Mrs. Root handed me the cheque for £3,000, and said she wished to lodge half. I filled the customary forms, took her signature, and gave her a passbook, all in the usual way. Then she told me she would like the other £1,500 cashed in notes of small value. She said she was a stranger to London, but that already she had discovered the difficulty of changing Bank of England notes. Being short of ready money, she had proffered a twenty-pound note in a shop. It was refused, and on asking for change in a bank which happened to be next door, the cashier politely informed her he was not permitted to change notes for strangers. She had, indeed, to go back to her hotel before she could get it done. She said she therefore wanted nothing larger than ten pounds, and at her further request I counted her out a hundred tens and a hundred fives. She stowed them away in a despatch case she was carrying. I pointed out that that was not a very safe way to carry so large a sum, but she laughed and said she guessed it was all right, that no one would know she had money in it. She said good day and went out, and that was the last I saw of her.”

“You noticed nothing in any way suspicious about her manner or actions?”

“Nothing whatever.”

“You say the lodgment was subsequently withdrawn? You might tell me about that.”

===Chinese===

「鲁特夫人把那张 3,000 镑的支票交给我，说她想存入一半。我照惯例填好单据，收下她的签名，发给她一本存折，一切照常。随后她告诉我，另一半 1,500 镑想兑成小面额的纸币。她说她初来乍到伦敦，却已经领教了兑换英格兰银行（Bank of England）纸币的难处。因为手头缺现钱，她曾在一家铺子里递出一张二十镑的纸币，铺子拒不收兑；又到隔壁碰巧的一家银行去换零，出纳员客客气气地告诉她，银行不许替生人兑零。她实在是回了旅馆，才把这事儿办成。所以她说，凡是大于十镑的面额一概不要；又应她进一步的要求，我点数给她一百张十镑纸币和一百张五镑纸币。她把钱收进随身携带的一只小公文箱。我提醒她，这样携带偌大一笔款子未免不大稳妥，她却笑道，她估摸着不妨事，没人会知道那里面装着钱。她道了声日安便出去了，那就是我最后一次见她。」

「她的举止行动，您没有觉察出任何可疑之处？」

「毫无可疑。」

「你说那笔存款后来提走了？讲讲那个经过吧。」

===Original===

“It was withdrawn in the sense that cheques were issued for almost the whole amount. The lady did not herself call again, nor was the account closed. There is still a small balance.”

French nodded.
