“I don’t know anything about that,” Mr. Jennings replied, “but they certainly did not sail with us. Perhaps, Inspector, they learned when they went on shore that you were after them?”

“Impossible,” French declared. “I did not myself know where they had gone until last night.”

He felt ruefully sure that the whole thing was part of the elaborate laying of a false trail, but he did not see that anything was to be gained by discussing this with the ship’s officers. He pushed his papers towards the purser.

“Can you recognise the parties from those, Mr. Jennings?”

A glance at the photograph sufficed. The original was undoubtedly that Mrs. Vane who had for a brief half-hour boarded the *Enoch*. And the description was that of Mr. Vane also. French was forced to the conclusion that his quarry had indeed, in the Captain’s words, been too many for him. He swore bitterly beneath his breath.

===Chinese===
“是故意的还是无心的？”船长插话道。

“这个我就不知道了，”詹宁斯先生答道，“但他们确实没有随我们的船开走。探长，会不会是他们上岸的当口，得知您正在追查他们？”

“不可能，”弗伦奇断然道，“直到昨天夜里，连我自己都不知道他们去了哪儿。”

他心里懊恼地认定，这整件事不过是那场精心布置的疑阵中的一环，只是同船上官员们谈论这一点，看不出能讨到什么便宜。他把材料推向事务长。

“詹宁斯先生，凭这些你能认出这两个人吗？”

照片只消一看便足够了。照片的原主，正是那位登上*伊诺克号*前后不过半个钟头的文恩太太；体貌描述也与文恩先生相符。弗伦奇不得不承认：借船长的话说，他的猎物确实棋高一着，把他将住了。他低声狠狠咒骂了一句。

===Original===
“You say they left some luggage in their stateroom,” he went on. “Could I have a look at it?”

“Of course. But, you know, they may still be here. On several occasions I have known passengers to miss the ship at Liverpool and follow on here. They may turn up at any minute.”

“If they do, so much the better,” French answered. “But I won’t bank on it. If you don’t mind, I’ll have a look at the luggage now. What time do you sail?”

“In about half an hour.”

“That will just give me time. Meantime I have a man at the gangway, and he’ll spot them if they come along.”

===Chinese===
“你说他们在特等舱里留下了些行李，”他接着说，“我能看一看吗？”

“当然可以。不过您知道，他们兴许还没走。旅客在利物浦误了船、随后追到这儿来的情形，我见过不止一回。他们随时可能露面。”

“真是那样，再好不过，”弗伦奇答道，“可我不能把指望押在这上头。你不介意的话，我现在就去看看那批行李。船几点开？”

“约莫半个钟头后。”

“那正好来得及。这工夫我有个人守在舷梯口，他们要是露面，逃不过他的眼睛。”

===Original===
There were four large suitcases in the roomy and comfortable stateroom set apart for the Vanes, as well as a number of articles of toilet and apparel which might well represent the first hurried attempt at unpacking. The suitcases were locked, but French soon opened them with his bunch of skeleton keys. And here he got confirmation of his theory that all this journey to Manáos was merely a carefully thought out plan. The cases were empty. Dummy luggage, brought in to bolster up the trick. But there was nothing in the cabin to give any hint of where the fugitives had really gone.

“I needn’t wait for them to turn up,” French said grimly. “Those empty suitcases give the show away.”

“I’m afraid it looks like it,” the purser admitted. “Sorry we didn’t know about it sooner.”

“Can’t be helped. That’s what we Scotland Yard men are up against all the time.” He bid the friendly purser good day and slowly left the ship.

===Chinese===
留给文恩夫妇的那间宽敞舒适的特等舱里，放着四只大衣箱，还有若干梳妆用具和衣物，瞧那样子很像是匆忙间初次开箱收拾了一半。箱子都上了锁，但弗伦奇用他那串万能钥匙很快便一一打开。这一下，他的推断得到了证实：这趟所谓马瑙斯之行，不过是精心设想出来的一步棋。箱子全是空的。假行李，抬上船专为把这套把戏撑足门面。只是舱内再没有什么东西，能透出这对逃亡者真正去向的半点风声。

“不必等他们露面了，”弗伦奇冷冷地说，“这几只空箱子把底细全抖搂出来了。”

“恐怕确实像这么回事，”事务长承认道，“可惜我们没能早一点察觉。”

“没法子。我们苏格兰场的人，成天对付的就是这个。”他向这位友善的事务长道了日安，缓步离船而去。

===Original===
But he did not leave the wharf. Though he thought it unlikely, there was still just a chance that the quarry had missed the ship and were following on. He would make sure.

But though he waited until the *Enoch* cast off and swung her bows round towards the open sea, there was no sign of any late arrivals, and when he had once seen the liner under way he turned disconsolately to his satellite.

“It’s all U.P., Carter, as far as this trip is concerned. They’ve given us the slip about proper. Goodness only knows where they are by this time; perhaps halfway to the States. Let’s find a telegraph office and report to Headquarters.”

A few minutes later French had sent a long wire to his chief at the Yard. Then at a loose end, he turned to Sergeant Carter.

“Well, Carter, what shall we do with ourselves now? Here’s ten o’clock and we can’t get back until the evening. We have the whole day to play round in.”

===Chinese===
但他没有离开码头。尽管觉得希望不大，猎物毕竟还有一线可能是误了船、正在路上往这儿赶。他要弄个确实。

可是他直等到*伊诺克号*解缆、船头掉转朝向大海，也不见一个迟到旅客的影子。眼看班轮启航远去，他只得垂头丧气地转向自己的随员。

“卡特，这一趟算是彻底砸锅了。人家把我们甩得干干净净。天晓得他们这会儿到了哪儿，兴许已走上去美国的一半水路了。找个电报局，向部里报告去吧。”

几分钟后，弗伦奇已给苏格兰场的上司发去一封长电。随后闲了下来，他转向卡特警士。

“哎，卡特，现在咱们拿自己怎么办？眼下才十点钟，要到晚上才回得去。整整一天，正好用来闲逛。”

===Original===
Except that he believed he could do with a bit more breakfast, the Sergeant’s ideas were nebulous. French laughed at him.

“It’s what I was thinking myself,” he admitted, “but it’s a bad time. These folk over here have no notion of what a good breakfast means, and it’s a bit early for their lunch. However, we’ll see what we can do.”

They went into a small restaurant and asked for coffee and ham and eggs. This proving too much for the waiter, the proprietor was summoned. He had a little English and at last understood.

“But yes, messieurs,” he cried, waving his hands. “The ham, the eggs, the omelette; is it not so?” He bowed low. “Immediately, messieurs. Will messieurs be pleased to be seated.”

Messieurs were pleased to be seated, and in an incredibly short space of time a smoking omelette arrived, garnished with chip potatoes and onions, together with coffee and delicious rolls and butter. To this the hungry men did full justice, and Carter’s estimate of the French, which had been low, went up several points. They took their time over the meal, but eventually it was finished, and the problem of how to fill in their time once more became insistent.

===Chinese===
卡特警士除觉得早饭不妨再补一点之外，主意一片茫然。弗伦奇不禁失笑。

“我自己想的也正是这个，”他承认道，“可惜时候不巧。这边的这些人对一顿像样的早饭意味着什么毫无概念，而吃他们的午饭又嫌早了点。不过，咱们试试看。”

两人走进一家小饭馆，要咖啡和火腿煎蛋。堂倌被这一下难住了，只得把老板请了出来。老板会几句英语，总算弄明白了。

“有的有的，二位先生，”他挥着双手嚷道，“火腿，鸡蛋，煎蛋饼，是这么回事吧？”他深深鞠了一躬。“马上就来，先生们。请二位先生赏光入座。”

二位先生果然赏光入了座。不多时——快得叫人难以置信——一盘热气腾腾的煎蛋饼端了上来，配着炸土豆片和洋葱，还有咖啡、美味的面包卷和黄油。两位饿汉把这顿饭吃得干干净净，卡特原本对法国人的评价不高，这一下涨了好几分。两人不慌不忙地用了这顿饭，可饭终究有吃完的时候，如何打发时光的问题便又迫在眼前了。

===Original===
“We might go round and see some of these coast places,” French suggested. “St. Malo or some of those. Or I dare say we could work across somehow to Dieppe and catch the afternoon boat to Newhaven. What do you say?”

Carter voted for going to the station and looking into the possibilities, and they walked slowly up the town, fascinated by the foreign life of the busy port. Havre is a fine city with good streets, shops, and public buildings, but it is not an interesting town, and by the time they reached the station, a mile and a half away, they felt they had seen enough of it.

An examination of the timetables showed that they were too late for Dieppe—the English boat would have left before they could possibly get there—and St. Malo, they discovered, was not in that part of the country at all, but miles away to the southwest. Trouville was only eight or ten miles away across the bay, but Trouville in winter did not seem an attractive prospect.

“Tell you what,” French said at last. “We’ve got an introduction to these French johnnies. We’ll go and look ’em up, and perhaps see something of their police station.”

Sergeant Carter, delighted with his superior’s condescension, hurriedly agreed, and a few minutes later the two men found themselves ascending the steps of a large building which bore over the door the legend “Gendarmerie.” Here French tendered his introduction, with the result that he was shown into the presence of and politely welcomed by the officer in charge.

===Chinese===
“咱们不妨绕过去看看这一带海边的地方，”弗伦奇提议道，“圣马洛（St. Malo）之类的。要不然，依我说，咱们也能想个法子横穿到迪耶普（Dieppe），赶下午去纽黑文（Newhaven）的船。你说呢？”

卡特主张先到车站去摸摸可行性，两人便缓步往城里走去，一路被这座繁忙港口的异国生活迷住了。勒阿弗尔街道漂亮，商店和公共建筑都体面，是座好城，却算不上有趣。等走到一英里半外的车站，两人都觉得这城看得差不多了。

一查时刻表才发现，去迪耶普已经来不及——不等他们赶到那里，开往英国的船早就开走了——而圣马洛呢，根本不在这一带，远在西南方几十英里之外。特鲁维尔（Trouville）就在海湾对岸，不过八九英里路，可冬天的特鲁维尔实在引不起人的兴致。

“有了个主意，”弗伦奇终于开口，“咱们手里不是有给这些法国老兄的介绍信么。去拜望拜望他们，兴许还能见识见识他们的警察局。”

卡特警士见上司肯屈尊俯就，喜出望外，忙不迭地赞成。几分钟后，两人已拾级登上一座大楼，门楣上题着“宪兵队”（Gendarmerie）的字样。弗伦奇在此呈上介绍信，随即被引至主管官员面前，受到彬彬有礼的欢迎。

===Original===
“I regret the Chief is out of town at present,” the latter said in excellent English. “He will be sorry not to have seen you. I hope that presently you will give me the pleasure of your company at lunch, and in the meantime let me know if there is anything I can do for you.”

French explained the circumstances. He would not stay for lunch, as he had but a short time since finished an excellent breakfast, but he would be most grateful if the other would tell him how best he could spend the time until his return boat to Southampton.

“That’s not until midnight,” answered the Frenchman. “You don’t know this country?”

“Not at all. It was just that if there was anything to see within reach, we might as well see it.”

“Of course, naturally. Well, monsieur, were I in your place I should certainly go to Caen. It is an interesting old town, well worth a visit. There is a steamer all the way, but you would scarcely have time for that; it is rather slow. I should recommend you to go to Trouville by steamer—it’s just across the bay—and then go on from there to Caen by rail. In the time at your disposal I really do not think you could do anything better.”

===Chinese===
“很遗憾，局长先生眼下出城去了，”这位官员用一口漂亮的英语说道，“他没能见到您，一定会觉得遗憾。我希望稍后您能赏光陪我共进午餐；在此之前，若有什么我能效劳之处，还请不吝见告。”

弗伦奇说明了缘由。午餐他就不叨扰了——一顿丰盛的早饭下肚还没多久——但倘若对方能指点他，在回南安普敦的船启航之前这段时间怎样打发最好，他将感激不尽。

“那要到半夜才开呢，”这位法国人答道，“这一带您没来过？”

“头一回来。只是想着，够得着的地方若有什么可看的，不妨顺道看看。”

“那是自然。呃，先生，我若处在您的地位，一定去卡昂（Caen）。那是座有趣的古城，很值得一看。坐轮船可以一路直达，只是您的时间怕来不及，船也慢了些。我劝您乘轮船先到特鲁维尔——就在海湾对岸——再从那里坐火车去卡昂。以您手头这点时间，我实在想不出更好的安排了。”

===Original===
French thanked him, and the other continued, “The steamer sails according to the tide. Today,” he glanced at an almanac, “it leaves at midday. You should get to Caen about two, and you could dine there and come back in the evening in time for your boat.”

At ten minutes to twelve French and his satellite reached the wharf, having delayed on their walk down town to consume bocks in one of the many attractive cafés in the main streets. They took tickets and went on board the little steamer. The day was cold though fine, and there were but few travellers. They strolled about, interested in the novel scene, and at last finding two seats in the lee of the funnel, sat down to await the start.

Midday came, and with leisurely movements the horn was blown, the gangway run ashore, and the ropes slacked. The Captain put his lips to the engine-room speaking tube, but before he could give his order an interruption came from the shore. Shouts arose and a man in the blue uniform of a gendarme appeared running towards the boat and gesticulating wildly. The Captain paused, the slackened ropes were pulled tight, and all concerned stood expectant.

The gendarme jumped on board and ran up the steps to the bridge, eagerly watched by the entire ship’s company. He spoke rapidly to the Captain, and then the latter turned to the staring passengers below.

“*Monsieur Fr‑r‑onsh?*” he called in stentorian tones, looking inquiringly round the upturned faces. “*Monsieur Fr‑r‑onsh de Londres?*”

===Chinese===
弗伦奇道了谢，对方接着又说：“船随潮水开行。今天，”他瞥了一眼历书，“正午启航。您两点前后可以到卡昂，在那里用了晚饭，晚间赶回来正好上您的船。”

差十分十二点，弗伦奇和他的随员到了码头——他们在下山进城的路上耽搁了一阵，在大街上许多诱人的咖啡馆里挑了一家，喝了几杯博克啤酒。两人买了票，登上那艘小轮船。天气晴朗却寒冷，旅客寥寥。他们四处溜达，看着新鲜景致，最后在烟囱背风处寻到两个座位，坐下来等开船。

正午到了。汽笛不慌不忙地拉响，舷梯撤回岸上，缆绳松了下来。船长把嘴唇凑上通向机舱的传话筒，可命令还没出口，岸上来了岔子。呼喊声四起，一个穿宪兵蓝色制服的人朝轮船奔来，拼命挥着手势。船长停住了，松下的缆绳重新绷紧，船上船下的人都屏息等待着。

那宪兵跳上船，顺着扶梯一口气奔上船桥，全船的人都眼巴巴地望着他。他对船长急速地说了几句，船长随即转身，面对下方瞪眼瞧着的乘客们。

“*弗——弗伦什先生*（Fr‑r‑onsh）？”他洪钟般喊道，目光询问地扫过一张张仰起的脸。“*伦敦来的弗——弗伦什先生*？”

===Original===
“It’s you, sir,” cried Carter. “There’s something up.”

French hastened to the bridge and the gendarme handed him a blue envelope. “*De monsieur le chef*,” he explained with a rapid salute, as he hastened ashore.

It was a telegram, and it contained news which, as it were, brought the Inspector up all standing. It was from the Yard and read:

> “Liverpool police wire Vanes went aboard *Enoch* and did not go ashore again. Mackay was watching ship for Henson and saw them. They must still be on board. Follow ship to Oporto or Lisbon.”

“Come ashore, Carter,” French cried rapidly, rushing to the side. The boat was actually moving, but the two men, jumping, reached the wharf amid the execrations of the Captain and staff.

===Chinese===
“叫的是您哪，长官，”卡特喊道，“有情况。”

弗伦奇急忙登上船桥，那宪兵递给他一只蓝色信封。“局长先生（*monsieur le chef*）发来的，”他急急敬了个礼，说完便赶着上了岸。

那是一封电报，里面的消息仿佛迎头一棒，把探长惊得呆立当场。电报是苏格兰场发来的，电文如下：

> “利物浦警方来电：文恩夫妇登上*伊诺克号*后并未再离船上岸。麦凯当时为缉捕亨森而监视出港船只，亲眼看见二人。他们必定仍在船上。请尾随该轮前往波尔图或里斯本。”

“卡特，上岸！”弗伦奇急促地喊了一声，直奔船边。船已经在动了，两人纵身一跳，落在码头上，身后是船长和船员们的连声臭骂。

===Original===
“Here, officer,” he called, beckoning to the gendarme, who had watched the proceedings with a horrified interest, “how do you get quickly to Headquarters?”

The man bowed, shrugged his shoulders, and indicated in dumb show that he did not understand. French hailed a passing taxi and pushed his companions in.

“*Monsieur le chef!*” he cried to the bewildered gendarme, producing and tapping the telegram. “*Monsieur le chef?*”

The man understood. A smile dawned on his perturbed countenance, and with a rapid flow of French he gave the required address. In ten minutes they were once more at the gendarmerie, French still clamouring for “*Monsieur le chef*.”

===Chinese===
“喂，长官，”他朝那宪兵招手喊道——宪兵一直怀着惊骇的兴致瞧着这一番折腾——“怎么走能最快到总局？”

那人鞠了个躬，耸耸肩，做着手势表示自己听不懂。弗伦奇拦下一辆过路的出租汽车，把同伴们塞进车里。

“*局长先生*！”他冲着那位一头雾水的宪兵喊道，掏出电报敲了敲。“*局长先生*？”

那人恍然大悟，局促不安的脸上绽开了笑容，一串飞快的法语说出了要去的地址。十分钟后，他们再度来到宪兵队，弗伦奇嘴里还一个劲儿嚷着“*局长先生*”。

===Original===
He was shown into the room of the same polite officer whom he had previously met.

“Ah,” the latter said, “so my man was in time. You got your telegram?”

“Yes, sir, I did, and greatly obliged to you I am for your trouble. But I can’t make head or tail of the thing. Those ship’s officers this morning were absolutely positive the wanted couple had not sailed.”

The officer shrugged his shoulders.

“Doubtless,” he said smoothly. “All the same I thought you should have the message, lest you should wish to follow up the steamer as suggested.”

“I have no choice,” French returned. “It is an order from Headquarters. Perhaps, sir, you would add to your already great kindness by telling me my route. With this confounded difference of language I feel myself all at sea.”

===Chinese===
他被引进了先前遇见的那位客气官员的房间。

“啊，”那位说道，“这么说，我的人赶上了。电报接到了？”

“接到了，长官，多蒙您费心，感激不尽。可这事我摸不着半点头脑。今天早上那几位船上的官员一口咬定，被通缉的那对夫妇没有随船开走。”

那位官员耸了耸肩。

“想必是的，”他圆滑从容地说，“尽管如此，我想电报还是应该送到您手上，万一您想照电报的意思去追那班轮船呢。”

“我别无选择，”弗伦奇答道，“这是总部的命令。长官，可否请您再添一分好心，指点指点我的路线？语言这么隔阂，真叫我如坠云雾。”

===Original===
The officer, who had seemed bored as to the movements of the Vanes, became once more the efficient, interested consultant. The obvious route, he said, was via Paris. It was true that you could get across country to pick up the international express at Bordeaux, but Paris was quicker and more comfortable. Fortunately, French had returned in time to catch the midday train to the capital. It left at 12:40, and he could easily reach the station and book in the twenty minutes which remained before that hour.

His time from the receipt of the wire until the Paris express pulled out of Havre station had been so fully occupied that French had not been able seriously to consider the message sent. Now, seated in the corner of a second-class compartment with Carter opposite, he drew the flimsy sheet from his pocket and reread it carefully. He understood the reference to Mackay and Henson. Detective-Sergeant Mackay was one of the best men of the Liverpool detective staff, and he was on a very similar job to French’s own. He was watching the outgoing steamers in the hope of capturing one Charles Henson, who with a couple of others had made a sensational raid on a country bank, and after murdering the manager, had got away with a large haul from the safe. French knew Mackay personally, and he was satisfied that if he had said the Vanes had gone on board and remained there, they had done so.

He wondered how it came that Mackay had not at the time recognised the Vanes as a wanted couple. Probably, he thought, the man had been so much occupied with his own case that he had not read up the particulars in the *Bulletin*, which, after all, was a magazine intended more for the rank and file than for men on specialised duties. However, the fact remained that Mackay had missed his chance, though his habit of detailed observation had enabled him to some extent to redeem his error.

But if it was true that the Vanes had not left the ship at Liverpool, what became of the statements of the Captain and Purser? It was not likely that these men could be hoodwinked over such a matter. They were experts; moreover, they were dealing with a ship with whose every part they were familiar. To the Vanes, on the other hand, the ship would be strange, and they would be ignorant of its routine. Under these circumstances it was absolutely out of the question that the pair could have hidden themselves on board. No, if they were there, the Captain would have known of it. French could not devise any explanation of the matter. The whole thing seemed a contradiction.

===Chinese===
这位官员对文恩夫妇的行踪似乎兴味索然，此刻又变回了那个干练而热心的参谋。他说，路线显然取道巴黎。固然也可以穿过乡间到波尔多（Bordeaux）去搭国际快车，但走巴黎更快也更舒适。所幸弗伦奇赶回来得正是时候，还来得及搭上开往首都的正午班车。班车 12:40 开，离那个钟点还剩二十分钟，他尽可以从容赶到车站、买好车票。

从接到电报到巴黎快车驶出勒阿弗尔车站，这一段时间排得满满当当，弗伦奇一直没能认真推敲电文。此刻他坐在二等车厢的角落里，卡特坐在对面，他把那张薄薄的电报纸从口袋里抽出来，仔仔细细又读了一遍。电文里提到麦凯和亨森，他心里有数。侦缉警士麦凯（Mackay）是利物浦侦缉队里数一数二的干员，手头的差事同弗伦奇自己的极其相似：他监视出港的轮船，想拿获一个名叫查尔斯·亨森（Charles Henson）的人——此人伙同另外两个同伙，骇人听闻地洗劫了一家乡村银行，杀死经理之后，从保险柜里卷走了一大笔巨款。弗伦奇同麦凯本人相识，他深信不疑：麦凯既然说文恩夫妇上了船并留在船上，那便断然不假。

他不明白，麦凯当时怎么没有认出这对被通缉的文恩夫妇。他想，多半是那人一心扑在自己办的案子上，没有细读《公报》上的通缉详情——毕竟那份刊物原是办给一般警员看的，倒不是给担任专门勤务的人预备的。然而事实终究是事实：麦凯错过了机会，亏得他有观察入微的习惯，才算多少弥补了自己的过失。

可是，如果文恩夫妇当真没有在利物浦下船，那船长和事务长的一番话又作何解释？这种事要想瞒过他们两位，恐怕难了。他们是行家，况且打交道的又是自家的船，一钉一铆都了如指掌。反观文恩夫妇，这条船对他们人生地不熟，船上的章程规矩也一概不知。在这种情形下，两人想藏匿在船上，是万万办不到的。不，他们若真在船上，船长断无不知之理。弗伦奇想不出此事的任何解释，整件事看上去自相矛盾。

===Original===
He had, however, to settle his own plans. The kindly French police officer had helped him by phoning the local office of the Booth Line and finding out the itinerary of the *Enoch*. This was Saturday, and on the afternoon of the following day, Sunday, the steamer was expected to reach Leixoes, the port of Oporto. She would remain there that night and the next day, leaving Leixoes about 8 o’clock on the Monday evening. Next day about noon she was due in Lisbon, where she would remain for two days. After that her first call was Madeira.

French had intended to meet her in Lisbon, but it now occurred to him that he might be able to make Oporto in time to join her there. He had bought a railway guide in Havre, and he now proceeded to look up the trains. The route, he saw, was to Bordeaux by the Paris-Orleans line, then on by the Midi to the Spanish frontier at Irun, and so by Medina and Salamanca to Oporto. The first through train from Paris after their arrival at 4:35 p.m. was the 10:22 p.m. from the Gare Quai d’Orsay, and this reached Oporto at shortly after midday on the next day but one, Monday. Oporto to Leixoes was only half an hour’s run, so he had six or seven hours’ margin. Oporto, he decided, was his goal.

They were fortunate in securing sleeping berths between Paris and Bordeaux, and there was a restaurant car on the train to Irun. They waited an hour at the frontier station, and French blessed the intelligence of Manning, who had had their identification papers made available for Spain and Portugal as well as France.

French on his trip from Chamonix to Barcelona had been amazed by the illimitable extent of the earth, but his feelings of wonder on that occasion were as nothing compared to those he now experienced. The journey from Irun to Oporto was absolutely *endless*; at least he thought so as interminable mile succeeded interminable mile, while day turned into night and night more slowly turned back into day. It was cold, too, through the high tableland of Spain—bitterly cold, and the two men could not get the kind of meals they liked, nor could they sleep well in the somewhat jolting coaches. But all things come to an end, and at half-past one on the Monday, about an hour late, the train came finally to a stand in the Estacao Central of Oporto. There was plenty of time, and the travellers went straight to the Porto Hotel for a short rest before setting out to find the tramway to Leixoes.

===Chinese===
不过，他还得定下自己的行程。那位好心的法国警官已经帮他把电话打到布斯轮船公司的本地办事处，问清了*伊诺克号*的航程。这天是星期六；第二天即星期日的下午，轮船可望抵达波尔图的港口莱绍恩斯（Leixoes）。她将在那里停泊一夜又一天，到星期一傍晚 8 点前后离开莱绍恩斯。次日正午前后，该轮应抵里斯本（Lisbon），在那里停留两天。此后第一个停靠港便是马德拉（Madeira）。

弗伦奇原打算到里斯本去同她会合，此刻忽然想到，说不定还来得及赶到波尔图上船。他在勒阿弗尔买了一本铁路指南，现在便动手查起车次来。他看出路线是：先沿巴黎—奥尔良铁路到波尔多，再转米迪铁路到西班牙边境的伊伦（Irun），然后经梅迪纳（Medina）、萨拉曼卡（Salamanca）到波尔图。他们下午 4:35 抵达巴黎，此后从巴黎开出的第一班直达车，是当晚 10:22 自奥赛码头车站（Gare Quai d'Orsay）发车的一班，后天、也就是星期一正午刚过便抵达波尔图。波尔图到莱绍恩斯不过半个钟头车程，这样他手里还有六七个钟头的富余。他拿定主意：目标就是波尔图。

两人运气不坏，弄到了巴黎到波尔多之间的卧铺，去伊伦的列车上还挂有餐车。在边境车站他们等了一个钟头，弗伦奇不禁连连称许曼宁的先见之明——亏得他，两人的身份证明文件不但法国通用，连西班牙和葡萄牙也都用得上。

弗伦奇从夏蒙尼去巴塞罗那的那一趟，已经惊叹过大地的无边无际，可那回的惊奇比起这一回，简直不值一提。从伊伦到波尔图这段旅程，实在*漫无尽头*——至少他是这么觉得的：望不到头的里程过了一程又一程，白昼熬成了黑夜，黑夜又更慢地熬回白昼。行经西班牙高原时又冷——冷得彻骨，两人吃不上合口味的饭食，在颠簸摇晃的车厢里也睡不安稳。但天下事总有尽头。星期一下午一点半，列车晚点约一个钟头，终于停进了波尔图的中央车站（Estacao Central）。时间宽裕得很，两位旅客径直去了波尔图旅馆（Porto Hotel），稍事休息，再动身去寻开往莱绍恩斯的电车。

===Original===
French was immensely struck with the picturesque, old world city, nestling on the steep, hilly banks of the Douro, and he marvelled to feel quiver at every horse-hoof the great high level Dom Luez bridge, which throws its spidery steel arch in a single span of nearly 600 feet across the placid river flowing far beneath. Then after passing down the steeply-inclined streets to near the water’s edge, he and Carter boarded the tram and set off seawards along a road skirting the right bank of the stream.

In spite of the business which had brought them so far, both men gazed with intense interest at the unwonted sights they passed, the semitropical vegetation, the long, narrow, four-wheeled carts with their teams of oxen, the mole constructed across some three-quarters of the mouth of the Douro to increase the scour through the remainder, then, passing a stretch of sandhills, they finally reached the houses of Leixos, with lying below them the harbour contained within its two encircling stone piers, and, blessed sight, the *Enoch* lying at anchor therein.

They made a bargain with a dusky boatman for what seemed to French a fortune of reis, and ten minutes later they had ascended the ladder and were once more on the steamer’s deck.

===Chinese===
这座古色古香、如画般的城池，依偎在杜罗河（Douro）陡峭起伏的两岸山坡上，弗伦奇看得心醉神迷；更令他叹为观止的是头顶高处那座唐·路易大桥（Dom Luez bridge）——一道蛛网般的钢拱，以近 600 英尺的单跨凌空飞架，横过脚下远处平缓流淌的河水——桥上每过一记马蹄，桥身竟随之微微震颤。随后，他和卡特顺着陡斜的街道一路下到将近水边，登上了电车，沿着河的右岸朝入海口方向驶去。

尽管是公务把他们带到这么远的地方，两人还是兴致勃勃地盯着沿途少见的景致：亚热带的草木，牛队拉着狭长的四轮板车，还有横筑在杜罗河口约四分之三处的一道防波堤——修造它是为了让余下的口门水流更急、冲刷更深。随后穿过一带沙丘，他们终于望见莱绍恩斯的房舍：房舍下方，是环抱在两道石堤之间的港湾，而那谢天谢地的景象——*伊诺克号*正锚泊其中！

他们同一位肤色黝黑的船夫讲定了价钱——那数目在弗伦奇听来简直是一笔雷伊斯（reis）巨款——十分钟后，两人已攀上舷梯，重新站在这艘轮船的甲板上。

## XIV Tragedy / 十四、惨剧

===Original===
Some days later Inspector French was once again sent for by his chief. The great man seemed in an irritable frame of mind, and he began to speak before the other had well entered the room.

“See here, French,” he greeted him; “here’s a fresh development in that confounded Gething case. Read that.”

French stepped up to the desk and took the postal telegraph sheets his superior held out. They bore a message from the Chief of Police at the Hook, which had been sent out at 8:27 that morning.

> “Captain of the S.S. *Parkeston* reports that tall, clean-shaven, white-haired man, apparently named Duke, committed suicide during passage from Harwich last night. Overcoat and suitcase found in cabin with letter addressed Miss Duke, The Cedars, Hampstead. Am sending letter with detailed statement.”

===Chinese===
过了几天，弗伦奇探长又一次被上司召去。那位大人物看来心绪烦躁，弗伦奇还没进得门来，他便开了口。

「你瞧，弗伦奇，」他招呼道，「那个见鬼的格辛案又有了新进展。看看这个。」

弗伦奇走到桌前，接过上司递来的邮政电报纸。那是荷兰角（the Hook）警察局长发来的电文，当天早上 8 点 27 分拍出。

> 「帕克斯顿号（S.S. Parkeston）船长报告：一名高个、面净无须、头发花白的男子，显然名叫杜克，于昨夜自哈里奇渡海途中自杀。舱内发现大衣与手提箱，并有致杜克小姐的信一封，地址为汉普斯特德雪松居。现随函寄上该信及详细报告。」

===Original===
French was considerably surprised by the news. Though he had never felt actually cordial towards the old gentleman, he had respected him for his kindly conduct towards his subordinates and for the sportsmanlike way in which he had taken his loss. But it was evident the man had been hit harder than he had shown. French recalled the details of their last interview, the merchant’s drawn, anxious face, his weary air, his almost despairing words, “I’m getting to the end of my tether. I see ruin staring me in the face.” At the time, French had not taken the complaint as seriously as it had now proved to warrant. Mr. Duke was evidently in difficulties which nothing less than the return of the stolen diamonds would solve, and French did not see how he could have done more to achieve that end than he already had.

“Unexpected, that, isn’t it?” the chief remarked, “though I don’t suppose it will really affect the case.”

“No, sir, I don’t think it will,” French returned, answering the last part of the sentence first. “But I don’t know that it’s so unexpected after all. Leastwise it is and it isn’t. I mean, I’m surprised that a man of Mr. Duke’s character should take that way of escaping from his difficulties, but I knew he was in difficulties.”

===Chinese===
这消息着实让弗伦奇吃了一惊。他对这位老先生虽说从未真正热络过，却敬重他待下属的和气，也敬重他遭劫之后那输得起的光明气度。可如今看来，这一击落在他身上，远比他显露出来的沉重。弗伦奇回想起两人最后一次会面的种种情形：这位商人形容憔悴、满面忧色，一副倦怠神气，还说过那句近乎绝望的话——「我快撑到头了。我眼看着破产正瞪着我。」当时弗伦奇并没有把这番话看得像如今证明的这般严重。杜克先生显然深陷困境，非追回那批被盗的钻石无以解脱，而弗伦奇也看不出，除了已尽的本分，他还能再做什么来促成这个结果。

「没想到吧？」上司说道，「不过我想这未必真会影响案情。」

「是的，长官，我想不会。」弗伦奇答道，先接了那句话的后半截。「不过说意外，倒也未必尽然。可以说是意外，也可以说不是。我是说，杜克先生那样性格的人竟会走这条路脱身，这让我吃惊；可他深陷困境，这我是知道的。」

===Original===
The chief raised his eyebrows.

“You didn’t tell me that.”

“The truth is, sir, that I didn’t take what the old gentleman said seriously enough. I met him last week in Piccadilly, and he appeared anxious to hear my news and asked me to have a cup of coffee with him. He was pretty down in the mouth then, saying he was getting short of cash, and near the end of his tether, and so on. He was looking pretty old, too, old and worn.”

The chief grunted.

===Chinese===
上司扬了扬眉毛。

「这话你可没跟我说过。」

「实话说，长官，是我没把老先生的话当得够重。上星期我在皮卡迪利（Piccadilly）碰见他，他急着要听我的消息，还请我喝了杯咖啡。那时候他就已一脸颓丧，说什么手头拮据、快撑到头了之类。人看着也见老，又老又憔悴。」

上司哼了一声。

===Original===
“As I say, I don’t suppose it will make any difference,” he declared. “But there’s that girl to consider. I think you’d better go along and see her. After all, she should have some warning before she sees it in the paper.”

“That’s so, sir. Then I shall go now.”

It was a job he hated, but there was no help for it, and having phoned to Miss Duke that he was going out on urgent business, he set off.

===Chinese===
「照我说的，我想这事不会有什么影响，」他声明道，「可还有那位姑娘要顾到。我看你最好去一趟，见见她。不管怎么说，总该让她先有个预备，别让她从报上才看到。」

「说的是，长官。那我现在就去。」

这差事他打心里厌烦，却无可推卸。他先给杜克小姐（Miss Duke）去了电话，只说自己有急事出门，随后便动身了。

===Original===
That his message had alarmed her was obvious. She met him with pale cheeks and anxious eyes, and once again the thought occurred to him that she knew something that she was holding back, and had feared her secret was the subject of his call.

But his news, when haltingly and with some awkwardness he had succeeded in conveying it, took her utterly by surprise. It was evidently quite different to what she had expected to hear, and the poor girl was terribly overcome. She gave a low cry, and sat gazing at him with eyes dilated with horror. The shock seemed utterly to have benumbed her, and yet French could not help thinking that her emotion contained also an element of relief. He was profoundly sorry for her, but his suspicion remained.

Presently she began to speak. Her voice was dull and toneless as she explained that she had known her father was lately terribly worried and unhappy, and that though he had made light of it, he had told her enough to show that financial trouble was at the root of his distress. He had said to her on one occasion that if only the insurance people would pay, things would be easier, but he had spoken cheerily, and she had had no idea things were so serious.

“When shall we get details?” she asked presently. “Should I go over to the Hook?”

===Chinese===
他那通电话显然惊着了她。她迎上来时脸颊苍白、目光惶惶，他心里又一次泛起那个念头：她知晓什么内情，却一直守口不言，方才还只怕他这一趟登门，为的正是她那个秘密。

可当他结结巴巴、颇费踌躇地总算把消息递了过去，她却是全然猝不及防。这消息显然与她料想会听到的全然是两回事，可怜的姑娘顿时垮了下来。她低呼一声，坐在那里直瞪着他，双眼因惊恐而睁得老大。这打击像是把她整个人都震麻了，然而弗伦奇又不由得觉得，她的激动里也掺着一丝如释重负。他由衷地怜悯她，怀疑却并未放下。

过了一阵她开口说话。她声音低哑、全无抑扬，解释说父亲近来忧烦不乐，她是知道的；他虽一贯轻描淡写，可透给她的话已足以表明，让他愁肠百结的根子是钱财上的困窘。有一回他还说过，只要保险公司肯赔，日子就会好过些，只是他说这话时语气轻快，她万万没想到事情已严重到这个地步。

「什么时候能知道详情？」过了一会儿她问道。「我该去一趟荷兰角吗？」

===Original===
“I fear there would be little use in that,” French answered, “and it would certainly be painful for you. Of course, I don’t wish to dissuade you; if you think it would be an ease to your mind you should go. But in any case would it not be better to wait until you read your letter? Besides, the report from the Dutch police may show that a visit is unnecessary.”

She thought for some seconds, then agreed. French explained that the documents might be expected by the first post on the following morning, and promised to take them out to Hampstead immediately.

“In the meantime, Miss Duke,” he went on, with real kindness in his tone, “it’s not my business, of course, but would you not be better to have someone in the house with you—some lady friend, an aunt, a cousin? Or Mr. Harrington? I mean, is there anything that I can do to take a message or send a wire?”

Her eyes filled with tears as she thanked him and asked him to telephone to the office for Harrington. It appeared that she had no near relations. She was an only child, and her father was now dead, and French knew that for many years her poor mother had been worse than dead, dragging out a colourless existence in a mental hospital at Otterham.

===Chinese===
「我怕去了也没什么用处，」弗伦奇答道，「而且对您必定是件苦事。当然，我无意劝阻您；您若觉得去了心里好受些，那就该去。但无论如何，何不等读了那封信再说？何况荷兰警方的报告也许会表明，此行根本不必。」

她思忖了几秒，随即依允。弗伦奇说明这些文件料想可随明晨头一班邮递送达，并答应一到便立刻送去汉普斯特德（Hampstead）。

「眼下这段时间，杜克小姐，」他接着说，语气温厚出自真心，「这本不归我多嘴，可屋里若是有个人陪着您，岂不更好——哪位女友，姑母，表姐妹？或者哈林顿先生（Mr. Harrington）？我是说，有什么要传话、拍电报的，尽管吩咐我。」

她眼含热泪谢过他，请他打电话到商行叫哈林顿来。原来她并没有近亲。她是独女，父亲如今已死，而弗伦奇知道，她那可怜的母亲多年来生不如死，在奥特勒姆（Otterham）一家精神病院里苟延着一段灰暗的岁月。

===Original===
When he had put through his call, French took his leave. There was nothing more to be done until the details of the tragedy were received.

As he sat in the tube on his way back to the Yard, he was conscious of some misgivings as to the way in which he had handled the interview. He had done his best to make it easy for Miss Duke. This was, of course, the natural and the kindly thing to do, but was it his duty? Should he not rather have used the news as a lever to startle some admission out of the girl which would have given him the information which he suspected she possessed. If he had allowed a promising clue to slip he had neglected his duty and injured himself. And his chief was no fool. He would unfailingly see the possibility and ask what use had been made of it.

But though French felt thus a trifle uneasy, he could not bring himself to regret his course of action. He was not only a man of natural kindliness of heart, but he had the gift of imagination. He saw himself in the girl’s place, and was glad he had not added to her trouble.

===Chinese===
电话接通后，弗伦奇便告辞了。在惨剧的详情送达之前，再没有别的事可做。

坐地铁回苏格兰场的路上，他心里对自己处理这场会面的方式隐隐有些不安。他已竭尽所能让杜克小姐好受些。这固然是人情之常、也是出于善意，可这是不是他的职责所在？他岂不更该把这条消息当杠杆使，惊出姑娘一句什么招认来，好把他疑心她掌握的那份情报拿到手？倘若他放跑了一条有眉目的线索，那便是失职，也是自误。何况他的上司不是糊涂人，必定会看出这一层，追问他用没用上。

弗伦奇虽说因此略有忐忑，却怎么也不懊悔自己的做法。他不但天生心肠慈厚，还有设身处地的想象力。他把自己放到那姑娘的境地里想过，庆幸自己没有雪上加霜。

===Original===
Next morning the report came from Holland, together with Miss Duke’s letter. The former was a long document giving very complete details of the tragedy. The essential portions of it read:

===Chinese===
次日早晨，报告从荷兰（Holland）寄到，杜克小姐的信也随之而来。前者是一份长文，把惨剧的细节交代得十分周详。其中要点如下：

===Original===
> “At 7:21 today a telephone message was received from the Harwich boat wharf office at the Hook that a passenger had disappeared during the crossing under circumstances which pointed to suicide. Inspector Van Bien was sent down to make inquiries, and he obtained the following information:
> “Some little time before the boat berthed, the stewards, according to custom, went round the staterooms to arouse the passengers. There was no reply from stateroom N, a single-berth cabin on the port side, and when John Wilson, the steward in question, had knocked a second time, he looked in. The cabin was empty, but bore evidence of having been occupied. The bed had been lain on, though not slept in, a large suitcase was on the floor, and various articles of a man’s toilet were scattered about. The steward, thinking the traveller, whom he remembered to be a white-haired old man, was perhaps on deck, passed on. About half an hour later he looked in again, to find things in the same condition. He was engaged until after the boat berthed, but when the passengers were going ashore he went back to the stateroom, and again found everything as before. Becoming anxious, he reported the matter to the chief steward. The latter accompanied Wilson to cabin N, and they made a search. They found a half-sheet of paper and an envelope propped behind the tumbler in the little wooden shelf above the washstand basin. The former bore the words:
> “The letter was addressed to ‘Miss Duke, The Cedars, Hampstead, London.’ Both note and letter are enclosed herewith.
> “The tickets on this route are dealt with as follows: There is no check on passengers leaving the wharf, as this would entail too long a delay at the gangway. On coming on board, passengers apply at the chief steward’s office, have their tickets either collected or punched, and get their berth numbers and a landing ticket. The landing tickets are collected as the travellers go ashore, and this constitutes the check that all have paid for their passage. On the occasion in question, 187 landing tickets were given out, and only 186 were collected, showing that one of the passengers who came aboard at Harwich did not go ashore at the Hook.
> “A search of the ship revealed no trace of the missing man, nor had anyone seen him passing through the corridors or on the deck during the night. The chief steward recalled his application for his berth, which had been reserved in advance, and remembered having noticed that the old man was absentminded, and seemed to be suffering from acute repressed excitement.
> “The suitcase was found to contain articles of toilet and clothing suitable for an absence of three or four days, but nothing to throw any further light on the tragedy. We are sending it to you for transmission to Miss Duke, to whom presumably it belongs.”

===Chinese===
次日早晨，报告从荷兰（Holland）寄到，杜克小姐的信也随之而来。前者是一份长文，把惨剧的细节交代得十分周详。其中要点如下：

> 「今日 7 点 21 分，接荷兰角哈里奇航线码头办事处电话，称有一名乘客在渡海途中失踪，情形指向自杀。已派范比恩探长（Inspector Van Bien）前往查究，其所获情报如下：
> 「泊码头前片刻，侍者们照例逐间特等舱（stateroom）唤请乘客起身。左舷 N 号舱——一间单人铺位的舱房——无人应门，当值侍者约翰·威尔逊（John Wilson）第二次叩门后，推门张望。舱内空无一人，却留有住过人的痕迹：床上有人躺过，虽并未睡过；一只大手提箱搁在地板上，男人的种种盥洗什物散置各处。侍者记得那位旅客是位白发老先生，心想他许是在甲板上，便走了过去。约莫半小时后他又去看，情形依旧。到船靠稳之前他一直不得空，待乘客们纷纷下船，他再回那间特等舱，所见仍是一般无二。他渐渐着慌，便把这事报了男侍领班（chief steward）。领班随威尔逊同到 N 号舱，两人搜检一番，在盥洗池上方小木架的玻璃杯后头，发现支着半张纸和一个信封。前者上的字样是：
> 「那封信写明寄『伦敦汉普斯特德雪松居，杜克小姐』收。便条与信现一并随函附上。
> 「本航线船票按如下办法办理：旅客离船登岸时不加查验，否则在舷梯口势必耽搁过久。旅客上船后到男侍领班办事处缴验船票——或收回、或打孔——领取铺位号和一张登岸票（landing ticket）。登岸票在旅客下船时收回，以此核验人人皆已付过船资。当日共发出登岸票 187 张，只收回 186 张，可见有一名在哈里奇上船的旅客，未在荷兰角下船。
> 「全船搜检一无所获，夜间也无人见过他走过通道或出现在甲板上。男侍领班记起他领取铺位时的情形——那铺位是预先订下的——并记得当时就留意到，这位老先生神思恍惚，似有一股强自压住的激动。
> 「手提箱内装有盥洗用具和衣物，合乎出门三四天的用度，此外再无可资进一步查明此案之物。现将其寄上，请转交杜克小姐——此物想必本属她名下。」

===Original===
When Inspector French had read the report he turned his attention to the letter. The envelope was square and of good quality paper, and the address was in Mr. Duke’s handwriting. French sat turning it over. He wondered. ... He would rather not, but on second thoughts he believed he ought. ... There might be something that would give him a hint. ...

He took a Gillette razor blade from his drawer, and inserting it beneath the gummed flap, worked it this way and that. In a moment the envelope was open, and he drew out a letter and cautiously unfolded it. It also was written by Mr. Duke, and read:

===Chinese===
弗伦奇探长读完报告，便把注意力转到那封信上。信封方形，纸质上好，地址是杜克先生的亲笔。弗伦奇坐在那里，把信封翻来覆去。他犹豫着。……他宁可不看，可转念一想，又觉得还是应该看。……里头说不定有点什么，能给他提个醒呢。……

他从抽屉里取出一枚吉列（Gillette）刀片，插进涂胶的封盖底下，前后一挑一拨。不多时信封便开了，他抽出信纸，小心展开。信也是杜克先生的手笔，全文如下：

===Original===
> “My Dearest Sylvia,
> “When you receive this you will have heard what I am about to do. My dear, I will not try to justify myself; I suppose I should be brave and fight to the end. But I just couldn’t bear the ruin and disgrace which face me. Even before the robbery things were not going too well. As you know, the war hit businesses like mine worse than most. Now, even if the insurance company paid, I shouldn’t get clear; I should still be many thousands in debt. Sylvia, don’t think too hardly of me, but I couldn’t face it. Loss of position, friends, home, everything—and at my time of life. I just couldn’t.
> “But chiefly I couldn’t bear dragging you down with me. You will be free from that now. Your mother’s jointure cannot be touched; it is hers—and yours. You will see that all expenses for her are paid, and the remainder will be yours. Of course the house must go, but you will have enough to live on. You will marry; I trust soon. Remember that it is my last wish and my last charge to you that you marry the man of your choice as soon as may be convenient. Though we have not always seen eye to eye, you have been a good daughter to me.
> “Dear Sylvia, try not to take this too much to heart. I face the future, if there is one, without misgivings. Though the way I take may be the coward’s way, it is the easiest and the best way for us all.

===Chinese===
> 「我最亲爱的西尔维娅：
> 「你收到这封信时，已经知道我要做什么了。亲爱的，我不想为自己辩白；按说我该拿出勇气，抗争到底。可横在我眼前的破产与羞辱，我实在受不了。其实早在遭劫之前，生意就已不甚顺手。你是知道的，那场战争对我这一行的打击，比谁都狠。如今就算保险公司肯赔，我也脱不了身，还得欠下好几千的债。西尔维娅，别把我看得太不堪，可我实在没法面对。地位、朋友、家宅，一切都将失去——又是在我这个年纪。我实在受不了。
> 「而最要紧的是，我不忍拖你一同下水。如今你可从这重累中解脱了。你母亲的遗孀产（jointure）动不得，那是她的——也是你的。她的各项开销由你照料付清，余下的便归你。宅子自然保不住，可你度日的钱是够的。你会嫁人，我盼着就在早晚之间。记住，把你早日嫁给意中人，是我最后的心愿、最后的嘱托。我们父女虽说未必事事投契，你对我始终是个好女儿。
> 「亲爱的西尔维娅，尽量别把这事太放在心上。我去面对那个未来——倘若当真有——心中并无惶惑。我走的这条路也许是懦夫的路，却是对我们所有人都最省事、最好的路。

===Original===
> “Goodbye, my dearest girl, and if there be a God, may He bless you.
> “Your devoted father,
> “R. A. Duke.”

Inspector French had a slight feeling of shame as he refolded this unhappy epistle and, working deftly and mechanically, regummed the flap of the envelope and stuck it down. He was disappointed to find that the letter contained no helpful information, and with a sigh he set out to bear his news to Hampstead.

Miss Duke and Harrington were anxiously awaiting him, and he handed the former both the report and the letter, saying he would wait if she cared to read them in another room. She remained calm and collected, but the pallor of her face and dark rings beneath her eyes indicated the tension under which she was labouring. She withdrew with a word of apology, Harrington accompanying her, and French sat thinking, wondering if a direct question, unexpectedly sprung upon them, might surprise one or other into some unguarded admission which would give him a hint of the secret which he believed they held.

===Chinese===
> 「再见了，我最亲爱的孩子，倘若世上当有上帝，愿他赐福于你。
> 「你挚爱的父亲
> 「R.A.杜克」

弗伦奇探长重新折好这封不幸的信，心里微微有些惭愧，手上麻利地、机械地给信封封盖重新上胶粘牢。信里竟没有一点于他有用的消息，他好生失望，叹了口气，动身把消息送往汉普斯特德。

杜克小姐和哈林顿正焦急地候着他。他把报告和信一并交给前者，说倘若她愿意到另一间屋里去读，他可以等候。她镇定自若，可脸上的苍白和眼下的乌青，都显出她强撑着的紧张。她道了声歉退了出去，哈林顿陪着她；弗伦奇坐在那里寻思，若是冷不防抛出一个直截了当的问题，能不能惊得他们哪一个失口漏出点什么，好让他窥见一丝他所相信两人共同守着的秘密。

===Original===
But when they returned some half-hour later, Miss Duke momentarily disarmed him by holding out her letter.

“You had better read that,” she said. “You may want to see it and there is nothing private in it.”

French was momentarily tempted to confess his action with the safety razor, but he saw that he must not divulge police methods, and taking the letter, he reread it and handed it back with a word of thanks.

“Did your father say he was going to Holland?” he inquired.

===Chinese===
可约莫半小时后两人转来，杜克小姐递出那封信，倒叫他一时没了章法。

「这个您还是看看好，」她说。「您或许要过目，里面并没有什么私隐。」

弗伦奇一时动了念头，想坦白自己动刀片拆信那一手，但他明白警察的手法不可外泄，于是接过信重读一遍，道了声谢，交还给她。

「您父亲说过他要去荷兰吗？」他问道。

===Original===
“Yes, it was one of his usual trips to the Amsterdam office. He expected to be away for two or three days. But I now think he had made up his mind—about—this—before he left. He said goodbye—”

She paused, her lip trembling, then suddenly flinging herself down on the sofa, burst into an uncontrollable flood of tears. “Oh!” she cried brokenly, “if only it hadn’t taken place at sea! I can’t bear to think of him—out there—” She sobbed as if her heart would break.

French saw that she had settled the matter of his procedure. In her present condition he could not probe her with subtle questions. There was nothing for it but to take his departure, and this he did as unobtrusively as he could, leaving her in Harrington’s charge.

===Chinese===
「说过，他是照常去阿姆斯特丹分号走一趟，打算离开两三天。可我如今想，他动身之前就已下了决心——下了——这个决心。他道别的时候——」

她顿住了，嘴唇发颤，随即猛地扑倒在沙发上，眼泪不可遏止地涌了出来。「啊！」她泣不成声地喊道，「要不是发生在大海上就好了！我一想到他——在那外面的海上——就受不了。」她抽噎着，仿佛心都要碎了。

弗伦奇明白，他的进退已被她这一哭定了局。她眼下这般光景，他再没法用机巧的问题去探问。除了告退别无他法，于是他尽可能不惹眼地走了，把她留给哈林顿照看。

===Original===
He wondered who would take Mr. Duke’s place in the firm, with whom he would have to deal if his efforts to trace the missing diamonds became successful, and determined to call at the office and make some inquiries. He therefore took the tube to the City, and some half-hour later was mounting the steps of the Hatton Garden establishment.

Mr. Schoofs had already taken charge, and saw his visitor in his late principal’s office. The business, he believed, would belong to Miss Duke, though he had no actual reason to say so. However, Messrs. Tinsley & Sharpe of Lincoln’s Inn were the deceased gentleman’s solicitors, and no doubt fuller information could be obtained from them.

“I came over last night, and am just carrying on in the meantime,” he explained, “and you can deal either with me or with Mr. Tinsley.”

“Thanks,” French answered. “Then I shall deal with you.”

===Chinese===
他想知道商行里将由何人接替杜克先生的位子——倘若他追查那批失踪钻石的努力终有所成，往后要打交道的便是此人——于是决定到商行去问个明白。他便乘地铁到伦敦城，约莫半小时后，已踏上哈顿花园那家商行的台阶。

斯霍夫斯先生已经接手主事，在他已故东家的办公室里接待了来客。据他看来，商行将来会归杜克小姐所有，虽说他也拿不出真凭实据。不过，林肯律师学院的廷斯利与夏普律师事务所（Messrs. Tinsley & Sharpe）是这位故人的事务律师，更详尽的情形，无疑可向他们打听。

「我昨夜赶过来的，眼下暂且照应着，」他解释道，「往后您同我打交道也行，同廷斯利先生（Mr. Tinsley）打交道也行。」

「多谢，」弗伦奇答道。「那我就同您打交道。」

===Original===
“We’re really closed for business today, you understand,” went on Mr. Schoofs. “I’m merely taking the opportunity to go through Mr. Duke’s papers and see how things stand. If only Harrington had had his partnership, it would be his job, but as it is, everything devolves on me.”

French, having replied suitably, made a move to go, but he lingered and went on:

“Unexpected, the old man going off like that, wasn’t it? I shouldn’t have thought he was that kind at all.”

Mr. Schoofs made a gesture of commiseration.

===Chinese===
「您知道，我们今天实际是歇业的，」斯霍夫斯接着说。「我不过趁这个机会理一理杜克先生的文件，看看家底如何。哈林顿当初若是当上了股东，这担子本该他挑，如今既然没成，一切便都落到我肩上。」

弗伦奇得体地应了几句，起身要走，却又顿住，接着说：

「老先生就这样去了，没想到吧？我怎么也想不到他是那样的人。」

斯霍夫斯做了个同情的姿势。

===Original===
“Nor was he,” he agreed, “but it’s not so surprising after all. You possibly didn’t see him during the last week or two, but I can tell you, he was in a bad way; very depressed, and getting worse every day. I don’t think he was well—I mean in health, and I think it reacted on his mind. He was worrying over the loss of his money.”

“Was he really bankrupt?”

Mr. Schoofs had not the figures, but he very gravely feared it. It was a bad lookout for his daughter. Indeed, it was a bad lookout for them all. It was hard lines on elderly men when they had to give up their jobs and start life again. It was that damned war, responsible for this as well as most of the troubles of the times. It had probably made a difference to the Inspector also?

“Lost my eldest,” said French gruffly, and turned the conversation back to the late principal. He was, it seemed, going to Amsterdam on routine business. He had no stones with him, and there was therefore nothing to suggest that his disappearance could have been due to other than suicide.

===Chinese===
「他本不是那样的人，」他表示同意，「可细想起来，也不算太出人意料。这一两个星期您也许没见到他，我可以告诉您，他那时候已经很不对了；意志消沉，一天重似一天。我看他身子就欠安——我说的是身体——而这身子又连累了心绪。他一直为丢了那笔钱发愁。」

「他当真破产了吗？」

数字，斯霍夫斯说不上来，但他深恐确实如此。对他的女儿来说，前景堪忧。其实对他们所有人来说，前景都堪忧。上了年纪的人丢了营生，还得从头再闯荡世路，实在是残酷。都是那场该死的战争——时下的祸患十之八九都得记在它账上，这一桩也不例外。它想必也改变了探长的境遇吧？

「我折了长子。」弗伦奇粗声说完，便把话头引回那位已故的东家。据斯霍夫斯说，杜克先生此行是例行公事去阿姆斯特丹，身上并未携带宝石，因此也没有任何迹象表明，他的失踪会出于自杀以外的缘由。

===Original===
French had not really doubted the conclusions of the Dutch police, but the death by violence of a man bearing a packet of great value is always suspicious, and he was glad to be sure such had not obtained in this instance.

His next visit was to Messrs. Tinsley & Sharpe, the Lincoln’s Inn solicitors. Mr. Tinsley was the sole surviving partner, and to him French was presently admitted.

It appeared that Mr. Duke had left everything to Sylvia, “Though, poor girl,” Mr. Tinsley added, “by all accounts that won’t be much.” Mr. Tinsley was executor, therefore any further dealings French might have about the robbery would be with him. Mr. Duke and he had been old friends; in fact, he had been Mr. Duke’s best man, he didn’t like to think how many years previously. He had been shocked by the change in the old gentleman when three days prior to his death he had called to see him. He seemed ill and depressed, and had said, “I’m not feeling well, Tinsley. It’s my heart, I’m afraid, and this confounded worry about money matters,” and had gone on to obtain the solicitor’s promise to look after Sylvia “if anything happened.”

===Chinese===
荷兰警方的结论，弗伦奇本无疑义，但一个身携重宝的人死于非命，总有可疑之处，他很高兴确定此案并非那种情形。

他的下一站是林肯律师学院的廷斯利与夏普律师事务所。廷斯利先生是仅存的合伙人，弗伦奇很快便被引到他的面前。

原来，杜克先生已把一切都留给了西尔维娅。「只是，可怜的姑娘，」廷斯利先生补充道，「听人说，那也不会有多少。」廷斯利先生是遗嘱执行人，因此弗伦奇日后若再就劫案有所交涉，对象便是他。他与杜克先生是多年故交；说起来，他还当过杜克先生的男傧相，多少年前的事，他都不愿去想了。死者去世前三天他去看望时，老先生的模样变化令他震惊：看似抱恙，郁郁寡欢，还说过：「我不大舒服，廷斯利。怕是心脏的毛病，加上这桩闹心的钱财官司。」随后又讨得律师一句承诺，说「万一有个三长两短」，要他照看西尔维娅。

===Original===
“In the light of what has since taken place,” Mr. Tinsley concluded, “I am afraid he had made up his mind then that suicide was the easiest way out, though I was terribly surprised and shocked when I heard of it.”

“I am sure of that, sir,” French answered as he rose to go. “Then if any further developments occur about the robbery, I shall communicate with you.”

He returned to the Yard, made his report, and when he had attended to a number of routine matters, found it was time to knock off work for the day.

===Chinese===
「如今事已至此，」廷斯利先生末了说，「恐怕他那时就已打定主意，觉得自杀是最省事的出路，虽然我乍闻噩耗时还是又惊又痛。」

「这一点我深信不疑，先生。」弗伦奇起身告辞时答道。「那么，劫案日后若再有进展，我会与您联络。」

他回到苏格兰场写了报告，料理过几桩例行公事，一看已到了当天收工的时候。
